===PAGE 2===
MEMORIAM
LESTER P. FOSTER
1923 2002
One of the regular faces will be missing when you go to vote at the next election. Lester enjoyed being a warden
and greeting people with a smile while guiding their ballots into the box. His greatest pleasure was sports. At
one time he was an avid tennis player and received several trophies. He belonged to the Senior Golf League at
Maplewood Golf Course and bowled with the Seniors at the Mason Bowling Alley. When his boys were young, he
was President of the Little League and helped coordinate many fund raising events. Lester was a regular at the
Tuesday afternoon bridge tournaments at the Eagle House Senior Center. He also enjoyed playing cards and
games with his grandchildren. For many years he was an accountant at Webber Lumber and for Guaranty Bank
in Worcester. He retired in 1985.
RAYMOND A. POWELL JR.
1944 2001
Ray was a lifelong resident of Lunenburg and operated R.A. Powell Construction, later changed to Powell Stone
and Gravel from 1969-2001. He served in the U.S. Army and was stationed in Germany. Ray, as most people
know, was very active in town affairs and was particularly generous with his time and made several donations to
varlous endeavors. He was very interested in the youth of Lunenburg and served for many years as the
president of Little League and Pop Warner. He was also a great friend to the Park Department and the updating
of its facilities. Through Ray's extreme generosity, a new Little League Field was Installed at Marshall Park as
well as a new fence around the lower field. Ray was involved in many other projects In Town that benefited all of
Lunenburg. He was someone that you could always count on if you needed help. It is fair to say that Ray was
"one of a kind" and will be greatly missed.
WILLIAM F. REED
1923 2002
Bill and his wife Nancy were married for 57 years and had two sons and two grandsons. Bill was a Navy Veteran
of World War II, Korea and Vietnam Wars. He joined the Navy in 1941 retiring in 1965 attaining the rank of E-9
Master Chief Machinist Mate, the highest enlisted pay grade. He then worked at Parks-Cramer Company In
Fitchburg. He was active in the Boy Scouts, Historical Society and a lifetime member of the Lunenburg
Sportsman's Club. Bill was a member and Chair of the Board of Assessors for fifteen years. During those years
he tirelessly worked to provide fair and equitable representation for taxation. Bill never wanted to spend a nickel
unless It was necessary. He truly was a friend to the taxpayer. Later he served briefly as a member of the
Finance Committee but his health was not cooperating and he was forced to resign. That didn't keep him from
expressing his opinion-one never had to guess where Bill stood on any issue. He will be missed.
GEORGE ROLLINS SR.
1914 2002
George Rollins Sr. came to Lunenburg with his wife Beatrice and their two children in 1956. In June George and
Beatrice celebrated their sixty-fifth wedding anniversary. George worked at American Can for more than 30
years. George also served the community in a variety of areas: as a Boy Scout Leader, good old-fashioned
census taker, past master of the Aurora Lodge of Masons, President of the Friendly Seniors, and member of the
Bowling League. George kept himself very busy but what he will be remembered for most is his infectious smile
and friendly hello on Election Day. George will be missed by all.
ROGER L. WILLIAMS
1941-2002
Roger L. Williams was born in Nashua NH and raised in Pepperell MA. He was employed as a teacher for eighteen
years prior to his appointment 1983 as Bi-Town Building Inspector for the Towns of Townsend and Lunenburg.
He moved to Lunenburg in 1988 at which time he was also appointed full time in the capacity of Building
Inspector/Zoning Officer for Lunenburg. He retired from his position with the Town this past year due to health
issues and made our town his home until his death in October. Roger's warmth and friendliness made him a
person one did not easily forget. His loyalty and dedication to the Town will long be remembered.

===PAGE 9===
BOARD OF SELECTMEN/SEWER COMMISSIONERS
The Board of Selectmen/Sewer Commissioners entered into two contracts for the next phases of sewer construction in town.
Residents will be having a perplexing task navigating around town during the next construction season, as contractors will be
working on the permanent traffic lights at the intersection of Whalom Road and Electric Avenue. Construction crews will also be
reconstructing and resurfacing Electric Avenue from the Leominster/Lunenburg town line to the intersection of Route 2A. The
construction on Contract II of the sewer system will also commence, with work beginning at the Whalom Lake rotary and a
second crew will also be working somewhere along Massachusetts Avenue. (Route 2A).
A number of properties have already connected to the recently completed phase of the sewer line, and It Is anticipated that
properties along this next phase will be able to connect before the end of 2003. The Board also negotiated for the purchase of a
portion of the Hartwell property located on Massachusetts Avenue, chosen as the site for the Public Safety Complex. This site
as well as the site for the new Primary School will be incorporated into this next phase of the sewer project.
The Board notes with regret the recent resignation of the first CAFO, William J. Gustus. Bill has accepted a similar position with
the Town of Lynnfield and has agreed to help Lunenburg on a consulting basis as the Town begins its search for the next CAFO.
In accordance with the Town Charter, this search begins with the CAFO Screening Committee and they will make
recommendations to this Board for the top candidates. During the hiring process the Board has appointed Town Accountant,
Karen Brochu to the position of Interim Chief Financial Officer and Chief Administrative Assistant, Laura Williams as Interim
Chief Administrative Officer.
Former Selectmen, Michael J. Sauvageau also resigned as a Board member and accepted the position of Building
Inspector/Zoning Officer due to the recent retirement of Roger L. Williams. The Board also recognizes the recent retirement of
Fire Chief Dennis Carrier.
Renovations were made at the Town Hall including a new heating/air conditioning system for the second floor and the painting of
Town Hall. A survey was taken at Town Hall to find out from the residents which color they preferred; White or Tan with Green
doors and shutters. The final outcome was to go with the color.
Two new appointments were made this year with Scott Glenny nominated as the new Fire Chief and John Rodriquenz chosen for
the new position of DPW Director.
On a personal note the Board would like to recognize the strength and commitment of former Board member Ronald Lamothe
during his recent battle with health issues. As III as Ron had been he attended nearly all of the Board meetings and continued in
his tenure as coach for many of Lunenburg's student athletes.
The Board extends a special "thank you" to the following: Lunenburg/Townsend Rotary Club for decorating the Gazebo at
Christmas and the Turkey Hill Gardeners for the Town Hall decorations and flowers in the upper common. The Turkey Hill
Gardeners were also Instrumental in the Installation of the irrigation system for Upper Town Common. This was accomplished
through a generous grant from the Sidewalks and Grounds Trust Fund.
We wish to recognize the recent passing of Roger L. Williams, former Building Inspector/Zoning Officer who passed away in
October. He will be remembered for his gentle demeanor and his willingness to assist property owners in navigating through
town government. He will be deeply missed by this Board and the community.
Your Board has enjoyed working in service to the Town, working with each other, and with you the citizens. They especially
want to thank our CAFO, Bill Gustus, for working so well with all town departments and committees, his expertise will be missed.
Please feel free to direct any questions to our office staff Laura Williams, Interim Chief Administrative Officer or Linda Douglas,
Administrative Assistant @ 978-582-4144, if they cannot assist you, they will forward your call to the appropriate authorities. Our
office hours are Monday, Wednesday and Thursday 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM, Tuesday 8:00 AM to 6:30 PM and Closed Fridays.
Joseph F. Bllotta, Chairman
Ronald Lamothe, Vice-Chairman
Steven M. deBettencourt, Clerk
Robert H. Bowen, Member
David W. Rodgers, Member

CEMETERY DEPARTMENT
The Cemetery Commissioners and the Superintendent meet regularly though out the year to discuss Cemetery business. The
Commissioners would like to thank Lionel Loiselle for his work in updating and computerizing the mapping system for the
cemeteries. He has done this through the Lunenburg tax work-off program. He spent many hours during the summer months
with string and tapes locating and plotting family lots. Work is continuing on the new areas. The following is a list of persons
who were buried this year.

BUILDING INSPECTOR/ZONING OFFICIAL
The Building Officials office is located on the second floor of the Town Hall. Office hours for the Building Department are
Mondays 7:00-3:30, Tuesday 8:00-6:30, Wednesday 8:00-3:30 and Thursday 7:00-3:30 PM; closed Friday. The Building Inspector
is available in the office daily and inspections are by appointment only. Michael J. Sauvageau became the new Town Building
Official/Zoning Officer in June of 2002. Harry Cullinan serves as the Assistant Building Inspector and Lisa Normandin is the
Administrative Assistant to the Building and Zoning Departments.
On a personal note we would like to recognize Roger L. Williams, the former Building Official, who passed away in October.
Roger had over 18 years of dedicated service to the Town of Lunenburg. Roger became well known in the Town for his quiet
manner and fairness to its citizens. Roger was a good friend to many in the Town and is sadly missed by his fellow employees.
Building activity has been steady in the residential districts Issuing 370 permits for the year 2002; 56 of these were for new
homes. Other permits Include additions, woodstove permits and general maintenance permits.
The Department has been very active in eliminating outstanding zoning violations and will continue to "clean up" current
violations. Please note that any complaints should be submitted in writing. The Building Department also recommends that
house numbers be posted on the dwelling and on mailboxes using four-inch Arabic numerals. This will be helpful in an
emergency situation, and for locating houses for inspection purposes. Don't forget to check smoke detector batteries; a good
rule of thumb to follow is to check them twice a year when you change the clocks.
Residents should be aware that Massachusetts Law requires that all persons, partnerships and corporations who bid or perform
residential contracting (reconstruction, alterations, additions, etc.) for work exceeding $1000.00 be registered with the
Commonwealth of Mass. This office will enforce this provision, however, you should ask your contractor about his/her insurance
and registration.
Any construction projects such as sheds, swimming pools, porches, decks, additions, re-roofing, window replacement, vinyl
siding etc. all require building permits, as well as verification of zoning setbacks. Any alterations or expansion of structures or
uses and pre-existing nonconforming parcels may require Zoning Board of Appeals approval. Please contact this office at 978-
582-4146 with any questions about this requirement or for any other questions you may have.
CABLE ADVISORY COMMITTEE
Lance May, Chairperson
In 1971 the Board of Selectmen formed the Cable Advisory Committee to act as its representative between the town and the
licensed cable provider. In 1997 the Board of Selectmen entered into a ten-year licensing agreement with Cablevision. This
agreement established a TV Channel, Internet and TV Equipment for the town, and upon its receipt, broadcasting of the
Selectmen's meetings took place from a studio in the Town Hall. Since the start of production, other programs have been
telecasted and shown throughout the town such as the recent plans for a new public safety complex, a Selectmen's debate and
the towns 275th Anniversary First Night Celebration on New Years Eve.
An addition to the Cable Committee is the LEAP Committee (Lunenburg Education Access Programming). The LEAP Committee
Is responsible for the production of the above-mentioned programs and provides a public service to the community. In addition
to the above-mentioned programming for 2002, the town changed cable providers. In November, Comcast became the new cable
provider with a promise to re-build the system. However, the Cable Committee has yet to see any updated plans. Another
complaint that was addressed to Comcast was its customer service response to subscribers. The Cable Committee will continue
to monitor these complaints or, any non-compliance issue and seek to resolve them with the cable provider for the benefit of the
subscriber.
I would like to thank Ed Dextraze and Chad Adams for their contributions to this committee throughout this past year.
CAPITAL PLANNING COMMITTEE
When the Capital Program was started approximately 12 years ago, this Town worked steadily to promote FY Plans of Need. The
Plans of Need brought our crucial safety services closer and closer to an acceptable standard of protection for our citizens. The
FY Plans of Need assisted our schools in securing capital expenditures, improvement and maintenance needs. The same plans
gave our government the tools to carry on their statutory functions more and more efficiently. The citizens of this community
accepted the cost on the average of $400,000.00 per year over a twelve-year period. Most of the citizens also realized that that
11
===PAGE 12===
figure was not enough to keep us current; approximately $200,000.00 more per year was more in line to meet the needs. These
identified needs were issues that could, through effective planning, ward off full-blown crisis. The Items on the needs list met
the criteria of Mandates, Safety, Need, Crisis and Cost Effectiveness. The past spending of approximately $400,000.00 met the
criteria of the "Ability to Fund" with revenues and with borrowing.
Then came the fall of fiscal revenues. By the time we reached Town Meeting in May 2002, we were only able to have a Capital FY
Plan of $182,500.00. Many items were put on hold. Capital Planning took the largest cut in the Town's spending. The CPC knew
the cuts were going to affect the issues of upkeep and replacement for our safety services, buildings systems and tools to
administer effectively.
The Committee began again in late Summer of 2002 to prepare for the FY Capital Plan for the May 2003 Town Meeting. The
preparation of the FY2004 Plan has been conducted in meeting after meeting, evaluation after evaluation and priority setting after
priority setting. This has left the Committee with presenting a plan that addresses primarily public safety items such as second
Installment of the ambulance, jaws of life replacement, police cruiser, and 1/2 replacement of fire hose and police radio system.
As of this writing, the Committee does not know what the bottom line figure will be under the "Ability to Fund" criteria.
The Committee understands the fiscal crisis. The Committee can state to you, the citizens, that there is no alternative but to do
bare bone minimum spending in this national, state and local economy; however, the Committee reiterates that the gains made
in the previous years have suffered a severe setback. In the future we will again have to go back to playing catch-up. Our old
adage, "little guy", does have to grow up soon.
The good news is that the citizens have spoken in favor of the Capital Program on two large ticket items. As of this writing, the
proposed Primary School has gone out to bid. The Public Safety Building Committee is well on its way to finalizing a design.
These two important projects relate to the prioritization set out in our Master Plan.
One of the items noted in that Plan is the renovation of the systems In our school buildings. The most urgent item is the
Elementary/High School heating system. The Town is living on borrowed time with the fifty odd year old bollers. CPC has been
aware of the problem and notes that taking any further chances with these boilers failing could be devastating cost wise. The
design work is taking place presently now and the Town will have to face the item on the ballot. The CPC is recommending that
the means of funding should be Exempt Borrowing as the item is a major capital expense.
Planning Capital Expenses, both In the FY yearly plans and the Capital Program, are important to the Town's present and future.
The process is good and needs to be continued S0 the needs are Identified. The Committee will continue to develop the plans
under the criteria as stated above and the prioritization of need. The "Ability to Fund Criteria" will be met as the economy
allows. In the meantime, the Committee seeks public awareness of the needs and in the future spending on capital expenditures.
Marion M. Benson, Planning Board (Chair)
Seymour Schnuer, School Committee
Steven deBettencourt, Board of Selectmen
Ernest Hunter, Citizen at Large
Elaine Murphy, Finance Committee
CENTRAL MASSACHUSETTS MOSQUITO CONTROL
The Central Massachusetts Mosquito Control Project (the Project) currently provides its services to 35 cities and towns
throughout Middlesex and Worcester Counties. The Project's headquarters or visits to field work sites may be arranged by
calling the office In advance. The Project practices Integrated Mosquito Management (IMM), blending state of the art methods
and techniques with expertise, experience, and scientific research to provide our member communities with environmentally
sound and cost effective mosquito control.
During 2002 the Project received 3,252 requests for service from town residents and officials. We scheduled four hundred and
sixty-six (466) adult mosquito operations in residential areas, and four hundred and twenty eight (428) larviciding operations. A
total of over 4,191 pounds of Btl (Bacillus thuringiensis Israelensis) was applied by helicopter in 2 towns, Chelmsford & Billerica,
and by hand throughout our service area to area wetlands to reduce the emergence of adult mosquitoes. This represents over
838 acres of wetland that was treated with this mosquito-specific bacteria, significantly reducing adult mosquito populations In
these areas. Twenty-six thousand, eight hundred and thirty-two (26,832) catch basins were treated with larvicidal briquettes to
control mosquitoes that seek out cool dark wet areas to breed, including the Culex mosquito, a major target for West Nile Virus
transmission. Four thousand, one hundred and eleven (4,111) culverts were cleaned in an attempt to eliminate unnecessary
standing water and reduce mosquito breeding. This work was done in conjunction with cleaning, clearing and digging of one
hundred four thousand, five hundred and ninety-six (104,596) feet of streams, brooks and ditches. This represents almost
twenty miles of waterways which were cleaned and Improved by Project personnel in 2002.
12
===PAGE 13===
The Mosquito Awareness Program which we offer to elementary schools and other civic organizations in our district has become
very popular. Project staff meet with students, teachers or concerned residents to discuss mosquito biology, mosquito habitat,
and control procedures. Much of the presentation is directed towards what children and their families can do to prevent
mosquitoes from breeding around their homes. Slides, videos, coloring books and other handouts make this an interesting
program. This program is tailored to meet the needs of the specific audience. As part of our effort to reduce the need for
pesticides we continue to expand our wetlands restoration program. By cleaning clogged and overgrown waterways, mosquito
breeding can be reduced and drainage areas are restored to historic conditions. Btl mosquito larvicide is used to treat areas
where mosquito larvae are found. We routinely check known breeding sites kept In our database, but also encourage the public
to notify us of any areas they suspect could breed mosquitoes. Our field crews will investigate all such requests and treat the
area only if surveillance gathered at the time shows an imminent threat of mosquito emergence.
Our goal is to handle all mosquito problems with education, wetlands restoration or larviciding, but we recognize that there are
times when adult mosquito spraying is the only viable solution. In such cases areas are treated with either hand-held or pickup
truck mounted sprayers if surveillance gathered shows sufficient numbers of adult mosquitoes present to warrant an
application. This program is available on a request-only basis, and the exclusion process allows residents and town officials to
exclude areas under their control from this, or any part of our program.
The Project's surveillance program monitors adult mosquito and larval population density, and is the backbone for prescribing
various control techniques. Rain gauges are set out and data is collected by our surveillance crews in an effort to predict when
mosquito breeding will occur. Specialized mosquito traps are deployed throughout the Project's service area to sample for
mosquitoes that may be transmitting mosquito-borne diseases. In conjunction with the Mass Dept. of Public Health we sample
in areas suspected of harboring WNV and other viruses.
Educational pamphlets are available to anyone interested in learning about mosquito control and the services provided by the
Project, and these items are routinely stocked in member Town/City Halls and libraries. We also have a website, www.cmmcp.org
that has extensive information on our procedures. This website has become a model for other Mosquito Projects, and has been
widely used throughout our service area and beyond.
We would like to thank you for your support during 2002 and we look forward to helping you and your community with its
mosquito problems in 2003 and beyond.
CONSERVATION COMMISSION
The Lunenburg Conservation Commission has seven members appointed by the selectmen who are charged with administering
the Massachusetts Wetlands Protection Act and the Lunenburg Wetlands Protection By-law. They are also responsible for
management and acquisition of conservation lands and assisting the public with wetland and ecological issues and concerns.
In 2002 the Conservation Commission continued to be committed to managing and preserving open space
Matthew Marro, Conservation Agent, successfully completed a Massachusetts Forest Stewardship Program with the assistance
of a $2000 grant from the DEM - Division of Forests and Parks. Over the years the Forest Stewardship Program has supported
about 50 town projects across the state. It has sought to encourage activities that bring community members into the forest for
learning and teamwork, at the same time making sure that ecological, wildlife habitat, recreational or sustainable wood
production goals are being met. The grant has been used to develop a Forestry Management Plan for the Small Town Forest, a
215-acre parcel of land owned by the town located on Mulpus Road. There is only one trall in this wooded parcel and the
Commission would like to expand the trail system, enhance the wildlife habitat, provide signs, Identify and explore the harvest
and bio-diversity Issues.
Joyce Hachey, Administrative Assistant, represented the Commission in the nominating process for The Squannacook Region
ACEC (Areas of Critical Environmental Concern). The focus of the project was to seek the Commonwealth's designation of
ACEC. An ACEC is an area or ecosystem formally designated by the Secretary of Environmental Affairs as containing
outstanding critical ecological and cultural resources worthy of protection and preservation. A total of 26 ACEC's have been
designated In the 26 years the program has been in existence. The designation directs state agencies to preserve, restore and
enhance the resources of an ACEC, such as public water supplies, open space, farmland and forest lands, historical buildings
and archaeological sites, wildlife habitat, and surface waters, wetlands and floodplains. The area, known as the Squannassit, Is
a 70 square mile geographic region formed around the Squannacook, Nashua, and Nissitissit Rivers. It extends from Groton
westward to Ashby northward to New Hampshire and contains extraordinary diversity of landscape and ecology. Secretary
Robert Durand announced the designation of the Squannassit Area in December.
13
===PAGE 14===
JJ Cote, Commission Member, worked with other members of the community in an attempt to pass the Community Preservation
Act (CPA). CPA is a new tool for communities to preserve open space, historic sites and affordable housing. Governor Cellucci
and Lt. Swift signed the Act on September 14, 2000. The CPA is a statewide enabling legislation to allow cities and towns to
exercise control over local planning decisions. It provides new funding sources which can be used to address three core
community concerns: acquisition and preservation of open space, creation and support of affordable housing, acquisition and
preservation of historic buildings and landscapes. Due to lack of support, the initiative failed to pass.
In the past year Roger Glichrest remained active as an Associate Member of the Commission as well as managing the
construction of a public access for Hunting Hill conservation land. He has worked with Eagle Scouts Scott Reinhardt, Nathan
LeBlanc and Daniel Osgood on their Eagle Scout projects. Scott cleared the brush and widened the path known as the Beaver
Trail in the Cowdrey Nature Center. Nathan constructed observation blinds to view wildlife in a nearby marsh. Daniel planned to
construct and Install signage at the Gus Russell Conservation Land but the project has zoning and access problems. The
Commission appreciates the effort and planning that Daniel put into his proposal as well as the hard work both Scott and Nathan
put into their projects. It is a pleasure to work with such dedicated youth.
Matt Marro and Roger Glichrest worked with the Hunting Hill Land Management Team on a land management plan for Hunting
Hill Conservation Land located on Hunting Hill Road. The plan was a coordinated effort with the Town of Shirley and the Division
of Fisheries and Wildlife. A trail connecting the parcels of land will be established. A memorial stone for Thomas Lawson, a
committed member of the Commission who passed away in 1996, will be erected at the entrance. The land was purchased in
1998 with a Self-Help Grant awarded by the DEM. The Commission would like to commend the DPW for their cooperation in
constructing the parking area, their efforts are greatly appreciated. A dedication ceremony will be scheduled for the spring of
2003.
Town projects have had an impact on the activity in the department. The sewer project, the public safety building land
acquisition, proposed school site locations and the new track and field all required review. Lake Shirley began to implement a
Lake Management Plan, which also required extensive review by the Commission.
There were 63 Notice of Intent hearings, 2 Amended Notice of Intent hearings, 26 Requests for Determination of Applicability. 59
Orders of Conditions, 2 Amended Orders of Conditions, and 26 Determinations of Applicability were issued. There were also a
number of violations, some resulting in Enforcement Orders.
Current Commission Members are: Chairman Scott Perry, Vice Chairman Rob Verge, Members - Richard Bursch, Jack Byrne,
JJ Cote, Joy Derner and John Lattanzi, Associate Member Roger Glichrest. Matt Marro continues his service as Conservation
Agent and Joyce Hachey continues as Administrative Assistant.
COUNCIL ON AGING
The Council on Aging is a municipally appointed volunteer board authorized under Massachusetts General Law, Chapter 40,
Section 8B. In 2002 the Council on Aging celebrated 35 years of service to the Town of Lunenburg. The Council's mission is to
advocate for Improved quality of life for all seniors through supportive services, programs and education. The Council's major
responsibilities include setting of local policy for the administration of elder programs and services, developing, coordinating
and/or conducting such activities, advocating for elders and educating the community-at-large about the needs and resources
affecting their lives.
The Council on Aging provides a gathering place to assist elders with maintaining a healthy lifestyle by providing various
services, meals, transportation, social, recreational, instructional, educational and health related programs. In addition to center
activities, we assist in helping the elder to remain safely in their home through other services and referrals. The director is a
mandatory elder at risk reporter working closely with all agencies.
A monthly newsletter provides up to date Information for elders. Monthly health screenings and other clinics such as flu
immunization are conducted through Nashoba Nursing and Hospice. Nutritious meals are served daily both at the center and
through the Meals on Wheels Program. Fitness classes such as, line dancing, senior aerobics, chair aerobics, and a walking club
are offered. Intergenerational programs with our schools continue throughout the year, including collaboration with School
Links Services for one on one-computer classes. Other educational presentations are sponsored throughout the year, many by
the Friends of the Eagle House, Inc. The senior center is open Monday- Friday 8:00-4:00 PM, for assistance call (978) 582-4166.
A handicap accessible van is available Monday through Friday to residents 60 years or older providing transportation to
shopping, medical appointments, part time jobs, visits to nursing homes and of course to all activities at the Eagle House. Non-
elder residents can apply if they are "transportation disabled". The Council on Aging also arranges medical transports to
Boston and Worcester facilities.
14
===PAGE 15===
In FY'02 a part time Outreach worker assisted 405 elders. The Outreach worker establishes in-home visits and is responsible for
intakes and referrals. A Friendly Visitor Program and the "Are You Okay?" program provides companionship and calls to elders
living alone. If you need a home visit, please call our Outreach worker at the senior center.
Trained and certified SHINE (Serving Health Information Needs of Elders) Counselors provide free assistance to elders with
health insurance inquiries, food stamps, or social security and pension questions and all health related insurance programs.
The senior center is a focal point in the community for the coordination of all elder services. We work In conjunction with Central
Mass Agency on Aging, Montachusett Home Care Corporation, Montachusett Opportunity Council, Montachusett Area Regional
Transit Authority, Nashoba Nursing and Hospice Service, Mass Councils on Aging, and the Executive Office of Elder Affairs.
In an annual report to the Executive Office of Elder Affairs we calculate the number of persons and the number of services
provided. Assistance was given to 1242 elders, representing a 16% increase over last year. Of the elders served 32% were over
the age of 75 years. In FY'02 there was a total of 23,683 units of service to both elders and non-elders. Volunteers contributed
3687 hours of service.
The Director chairs the Senior Citizen Property Tax Work-Off Committee established at the May 1999 Town Meeting which finds
eligible senior citizens for job placements requested by town departments. Seniors who are eligible for this program can receive
up to a $500 abatement on their property taxes.
Theresa Longsjo presently serves as chairperson, with Robert B. Bowen as Vice Chair. Other members in FY'02 were Edith
Carey, Jane Doyle, Robert Fickett, Edith Gay, Roland LeBlanc, Barbara Reynolds, Paul Shattuck and Judith Tarbell. Barbara
Reynolds and Paul Shattuck have since left the board and were replaced by Shella Lumi and A. Bud Ruggles.
Present staff consists of: Jeanne DeBruin, Director; Susan Doherty, Principal Clerk; Yvonne Wright, Outreach Worker; Ethel
Mattson, Meal Site Manager; Gerald Beloin and Donald Paradise Van Drivers; Virginia Blair, Elder Service Corps Worker; and
George Pugsley, Custodian. Eagle House new Email address: Eaglehouse25@aol.com
DIRECTOR OF FACILITIES AND GROUNDS
John Londa, Director
In FY 03, voters approved the funding for a new public safety building and a new building committee was formed to bring the
final design to completion with an eye toward reducing the cost of the proposed building. Planning for a proposed library
replacement project continued with discussions between the Library Trustees and the School Committee on using the school
field between the Brooks House and Memorial Drive as the site for the new library. The high placement of the Town of Lunenburg
on the state grant waiting list made It very likely that the project might come before the voters in May 2003 for a decision.
Attempts have been made by the Town to delay the need to put this project to a vote this fiscal year. The architectural firm of
Margo Jones Associates completed design for a new primary school. Bidding for the construction of the project began in
February 2003 and is expected to be complete by April 2, 2003 with construction beginning during late spring of 2003.
The exterior preservation project for Lunenburg Town Hall which began work in FY02 continued into FY03 with the roof
replacement completed by Greenwood Industries, the clock restoration by Target New England Inc. and the painting and repairs
by Roumbakis Contracting Inc. The Town Hall returned to a traditional historic paint palette modeled on the 1867-1868 historic
colors scheme. The Installation of shutters on the Town Hall was delayed until April 2003 due to difficulties with getting
approved submittals for the shutters and the severity of the winter weather. The shutter Installation will mark the completion of
the restoration project.
The heating system at Ritter Memorial Library continues to be a major concern following the discovery of a leak In the end
section of the boller. The Capital Planning Committee deferred replacement of the boller.
With two building projects, the Public Safety Building and Lunenburg Primary School, scheduled to move forward in FY04,
planning for the future of the buildings being vacated in the Town Center will begin to be considered in the remainder of FY03
and into FY04.
School Facilities
The focus of the school maintenance plan for FY03 was to prepare for and initiate renovations to heating systems at Turkey HIII
Middle School and heating and ventilation system renovations at the Passios Elementary School and Lunenburg High. A
$275,000 renovation project for the Turkey Hill Middle School heating system was approved at a Special Town Meeting. In
15
===PAGE 16===
October 2002 the School Department was notified that a federal grant for $178,000 was awarded to cover 65% of the cost of this
project. Norian/Siani Engineers of Waltham, MA was selected to be the designer for this project. Additionally $105,000 was
appropriated at the Special Town Meeting for design costs associated with the HVAC renovations of the elementary and high
School. Norian/Siani Engineers were also awarded the contract for that work. In the May 2003 town election, this project is
expected to be voted on for funding.
In Fiscal Year 04, the focus of capital maintenance will be prepare a energy performance contract proposal for all schools.
Particular emphasis will be to upgrade lighting at all buildings to high efficiency lighting.
A multiyear program to bring all schools up to compliance with the Americans with Disabilities Act is likely stretch into FY05 due
to a lack on funding.
The Lunenburg Track and Field Athletic Association continued site preparation for the building of a soccer and track facility
between the High School and the Middle School. Work completed includes bringing the area to the planned grade, subsurface
drainage, the installation of water holding tank and preparation for the infield surface. Work is expected to continue into FY04.
High School: In Fiscal Year 03, extensive work on the heating control systems was performed as unit ventilation equipment had
frequent failures. Replacement unit ventilators are planned for the heating and ventilation renovations.
Middle School: Major work completed in FY03 included the replacement of the cafeteria lighting and the heating renovation
project.
Elementary School: Numerous minor repairs were completed at the elementary school during FY03. The only major work
completed was the emergency replacement of the electrical feed to the school with an overhead service in February 2003.
Primary School: Concerns about the age of electrical wiring and condition of the heat distribution system at the Primary School
remain. Leaking condensation return lines for the heating system required numerous highly disruptive minor repairs.
FINANCE COMMITTEE
As per the Town Charter, the Finance Committee worked with the Chief Administrative and Financial Officer (CAFO) to produce a
budget for fiscal year 2003. The budget that was presented to the Town Meeting by the CAFO was $18,524,217.00, or an increase
over FY'02 of 7.44%. Although the budget was not recommended by the Finance Committee, Town Meeting voted approval.
In the ensuing months, and currently, Massachusetts is experiencing a fiscal crisis due to a structural budget shortfall. As a
result, Local Aid for FY'03 was reduced by .75%. At the same time as the State announced reductions in Local Aid, and due to
circumstances beyond control, Lunenburg began experiencing deficits in the Town Budget. For that reason, all Departments
and Committees were asked to freeze discretionary spending and propose a plan for 1.5% from their FY'03 budgets.
FY'04 will prove to be another difficult year. As of late February, the Governor announced that Local Aid will be cut by 5%. Since
this Committee and the Board of Selectmen had anticipated and planned for at least a 5% reduction in State Aid, Departments
and Committees had already been asked to prepare a budget to reflect this reduction.
At a Special Town Meeting on October 1, 2002, the Finance Committee recommended approval of an article to appropriate
$5,800,000 for the design and construction of a Public Safety Complex. This article was subject to the passage of a Debt
Exclusion override of Proposition 2½ at a special election. At that special election voters chose to pass the capital facility
project by a wide margin.
Along with the recommendation of this project, and in the Interest of long range planning, the CAFO and the Finance Committee
recommended that Town Meeting vote to establish a Capital Improvement Trust Fund and a Bond and Note Proceeds Investment
Fund. Town Meeting approved both articles and these matters are currently in front of the State Legislature.
It was with regret that we learned that the Chief Administrative and Financial Officer, William J. Gustus, planned to resign in
November 2002. As an interim measure the Finance Committee, two members of the Board of Selectmen, Interim Chief
Administrative Officer (Laura Williams) and Interim Chief Financial Officer (Karen Brochu) will collectively prepare the FY'04
budget for your approval in May 2003.

===PAGE 17===
BOARD OF HEALTH
George Emond, Chairman
As of this writing, February 22, 2003 the Board of Health has experienced a relatively quiet year. As with all town Boards and
departments, we are feeling extreme pressure to cut costs. Our Board members serve the town completely without
compensation of any kind, and since nearly 90 percent of our local budget of about $19,000 consists of salary for our one
employee, our only remaining option to cut costs is to reduce the number of hours our office would be available to assist the
public. Regardless of the final outcome of the budget crisis, please be assured that the Board of Health will do all in its power to
continue to provide the services you are entitled to and most certainly deserve from your elected officials.
We were gratified when minor problems which delayed the opening of the sewer line in the Whalom District were corrected
allowing use of the system. We rest easier in the knowledge that septic system problems in this well settled section of town line
are at last a thing of the past.
Following a trend noted last year, incidents of rabid animals within the town continued to decline. Activity in the West Nile virus
arena slowed considerably at both the State and local level. While the Department of Public Health continued to request
notification of reports of dead birds and other animals, their requests for carcasses for testing purposes declined considerably.
There have been no reports of diseased birds or animals in Lunenburg so far this year. Several requests for permits related to
Beaver control were Issued. Rabid Animal incidents continue to decline while reports and publicity over West Nile Virus in birds
appeared to ease throughout the region. Well being clinics for children, adults, our elderly as well as the dental program offered
in our school system continue as always.
Our mission is to serve you, the Citizens of Lunenburg. If you have a problem or require information that you believe we may be
able to provide, please contact our office at 978-582-4135 for assistance.
NASHOBA ASSOCIATED BOARDS OF HEALTH
Nashoba Associated Boards of Health continues to serve the local Board of Health in meeting the challenges of environmental and
public health needs in Lunenburg. In 2002 particular efforts were made to respond to a series of new service demands in the
Environmental Division while adjusting to changes in the Nursing Division created by national trends in health care. Some of the new
service demands included:
Response area wide to the bioterrorism threat following the 9/11/01 attack by answering inquiries from other local
departments, through coordination with the Massachusetts Department of Public Health (MDPH) on its Citizens
Advisory Committee in developing the scope of their $13 million dollar Centers for Disease Control grant to better
prepare the public health response to future events. Updated Nashoba internet web site to respond to need for
information. (See nashoba.org)
Response to the bioterrorism threat through coordination and communication with the Centers for Disease
Control, the MDPH, and the Massachusetts Emergency Management Agency.
Response to the West Nile Encephalitis(WNV) surveillance effort through coordination with MDPH, through the
collection of suspected bird samples, submittal to the MDPH lab, and follow-up when the lab results were obtained.
Response to health related Impacts of beavers through coordination with the local Board of Health in the issuance
of the necessary permits.
Response to state mandated changes in regulations requiring more frequent beach sampling through sample
collection, submittal for analysis, and follow-up when results were obtained.
We look forward to continuing our work with Lunenburg's Board of Health. Included in the day-to-day work of Nashoba in 2002 were
the following:
Through membership in the Association Lunenburg benefited from the services of Nashoba staff Including:
Registered Sanitarians, Certified Health Officers, Registered Nurses, Registered Physical & Occupational
Therapists, Licensed Social Workers, Certified Home Health Aides, and Registered Dental Hyglenists
Nashoba Nursing Service Is the regional vaccine depot for MDPH. As such it is a valuable resource for the
regular distribution of vaccines to physicians in the area, provides regular public Immunization clinics, and is
positioned and equipped to provide for larger scale immunizations should MDPH require them.
Provided health education programs in collaboration with the Lunenburg Council on Aging.
Collaborated with Montachusett Home Care around elders at risk and other safety issues.
Responded to and assessed mental health concerns through crisis Intervention and teamwork with other
community resources Including the Lipton Center.

Environmental Health Department
Environmental Information Responses
Lunenburg Office (days)
98
The Nashoba sanitarian is generally scheduled to be available for the public twice a week on Monday and Wednesday
mornings at the Lunenburg Board of Health Office. Other meetings occur informally.
This does not reflect the daily calls handled by the three Nashoba secretaries during daily business hours.
Food Service Licenses & Inspections
108
Nashoba annually mails out and receives application from both restaurants and retail food businesses. Licenses are
renewed for existing facilities. Plans are submitted and reviewed prior to initial licensing. Most licensees are inspected
at a minimum twice a year. Where deficiencies are found, a re-inspection is scheduled to Insure compliance. When a
complaint from the public is received an inspection is also conducted. During this Inspection health factors of food
handlers is also investigated, and where appropriate medical consultation and laboratory testing may be required.
Beach/Camp/School Inspections
17
Camps are inspected at least annually at opening for compliance with State Sanitary Code, Chapter IV, 105CMR430.00.
Public swimming beaches are sampled for water quality every week during the summer and more often if a problem is
suspected.
Housing & Nuisance Investigations
20
Nashoba, as agent for the local Board of Health, inspects dwellings for conformance with State Sanitary Code, 105 CMR
410.00, Issues orders for correction, and re-inspect for compliance. Similarly, complaints from residents concerning
unsanitary conditions or pollution events are investigated.
Septic System Test Applications
76
Applications from residents proposing to build or upgrade a septic system are accepted, a file created, and testing
dates are coordinated with the applicants engineer.
Septic System Lot Tests
63
Nashoba sanitarian witnesses soll evaluations, deep hole tests, and, usually on a separate date, percolation tests

Communicable Disease Reporting & Control
Nashoba's Nursing Service & Environmental Health Department work together to meet the local Board of Health's
responsibilities under the law to do the following:
Investigate and control the spread of communicable diseases within Lunenburg (MGL Chap111, Sec 6, 7, and
92-116). Nashoba works with the Massachusetts Department of Public Health (MDPH) in this area.
Receive and process reports from physicians concerning cases of diseases "dangerous to the public health"
as defined by MDPH (MGL Chap111, Sec6)
Notify MDPH Division of Epidemiology and Immunization within 24 hours of receiving notice of any case
"dangerous to the public health".
Receive reports and undertake follow-up as necessary regarding certain food borne and waterborne diseases
and diseases being monitored by the MDPH.
Receives reports, Investigates and conducts follow-up on all incidences Involving positive rabies results.

HIGHWAY DEPARTMENT
John M. Rodriquenz, DPW Director
A formal consolidation of personnel and equipment has combined the Highway, and Sewer departments together with the
Cemetery, Parks, and Facilities divisions into a single Department of Public Works. This restructuring is built upon a strong
foundation of teamwork and cooperation already in place in order to provide a service to residents that is more economical and
efficient. Sharing of resources is necessary to maintain Town properties at a high level and to oversee an increasingly
expansive infrastructure.
We appreciate the efforts of the Capital Plan Committee and the generosity and support of the residents that allowed for the
procurement of the following Items: one ton pick-up truck with plow, a wood and brush chipper, and a six-wheel dump truck with
plow and sander attachments. The purchase of new equipment continues our vehicle rotation program and enhances
Interdepartmental use.
The following is a breakdown of line item allotments and expenditures for the fiscal year:
VEHICLE MAINTENANCE
All public works vehicles, fire apparatus, and police cruisers are fueled, serviced and repaired through this program centralized
at the highway garage. The Police Department was allocated $20,000 and spent $29,264. The Fire Department expended $9,933
of their $10,000 allocation. The School Department also exceeded their allocation of $3000 by incurring expenses of $5,014. The
Highway Division was appropriated $108,093 an expended $83,700, the balance being used to cover the overages of other
departments.
TOWN BARN
The Highway Division facility is located at 520 Chase Road. Costs to operate the physical plant; heating oil, electricity, telephone,
janitorial supplies, office equipment, and postage totaled $13,470 and $9,898 was encumbered to rebuild, in part, the
underground fuel and gasoline storage tanks.
TREE REMOVAL
As tree warden, the Public Works Director oversees all work done to trees within the Town right of way. Many problematic trees
must be handled by a professional service due to their size or location. Whenever possible DPW employees will work with the
sub-contractor or the power company in order to reduce the cost. The entire $9,000 appropriation was expended.
TRAFFIC SIGNS AND DEVICES
Electricity charges for caution and school zone flashers, and the traffic signals at the four corners of Whalom Rd. are paid from
this account. Street line painting, crosswalks, stop bars, and parking spaces are also a part of this line item as well as
standardized warning and street signs. Because of the sewer project and other road construction only $3,300 was spent the
balance was encumbered to the next year at which time final paving will be completed.
GENERAL HIGHWAY MAINTENANCE
The cost of paving and patching materials, drainage supplies, roadside mowing, and street sweeping are funded through this
line. Also the purchase of hand tools, small equipment Items and the subcontracting of tasks beyond the scope of the DPW and
not covered by State funding is paid for through this account. Because the fiscal overlaps the construction season money is
often encumbered to the next season. Such was the case this year: $100,000 was the budgeted amount with $48,000 brought
forward. A total of $131,219 was used ad an additional $17,3000 was forwarded to the next fiscal year.
LABOR AND OVERTIME
An amount totaling $270,711 was budgeted to cover wages for Highway Division payroll including clerical staff, lead mechanic,
and one assistant mechanic. The total amount was expended. Emergency calls (excluding winter operations) paid at an
overtime rate totaled $2,250 exceeding the $2,000 allotment.
SNOW REMOVAL
During winter months, clearing the roadways of ice and snow is the primary function of the DPW. The department is in a
cooperative with fourteen other communities in order to insure the best possible price per ton. Sand that is mixed with salt to
treat roadways is screened by town personnel and hauled to the highway garage during the summer. During winter weather
emergency subcontractors and public works employees combine over twenty pieces of snow fighting equipment to clear and
maintain the 100 miles of roadways, sidewalks, and parking lots throughout the Town. An appropriation of $135,000 was totally
expended but did not go into deficit. Clearly an unusually mild winter season.
SEWER CONSTRUCTION
Phase I of Contract I sewer installation proceeded throughout the entire construction season within the Whalom area of town.
The main line piping was completed with house laterals installed to each property line. A major pumping station was built on
Dana Street that will in the future become an integral part of Contract II as well. Whalom Road was milled and repaved and the
sidewalk running the entire length of the roadway was completely rebullt. Any of the side streets entering Whalom Road that
were not repaved will be completed in the Spring of 2003.

commissioners, and every one of my fellow town employees for their continued support. The sense of teamwork is strong as we
strive to provide the residents of our community with the best possible Department of Public Service.
INSPECTOR OF WEIGHTS AND MEASURES
Joseph A. Quinn
Having been appointed in mid November, which is at the end of the inspection year, and receiving no documentation or tangible
evidence of previous inspections, I cannot report to you in the standard format by listing statistical data such as devices and
commodities inspected and savings to consumers and merchants Impacted by this department. I am reporting by exception,
concentrating on what has been done thus far and plans for the coming year.
To date, I have assessed the departments situation, developed an action plan to ensure a successful program and compliance
with the Massachusetts General Laws. I have received the department's equipment and submitted it to the State Standards
Laboratory for testing and certification. I have ordered approval seals, business cards and an identification card. I have
submitted an updated fee schedule for approval which should offset the department's budget by bringing in revenue as allowed
by law. Security seals and a hand press, both used to prevent device tampering must be acquired. I have developed a yearly
Inspection schedule and will begin inspections in the coming days. During the year I will continue to attend training sessions
and further enhance certification as an inspector by adding additional disciplines.
I wish to thank all of the town officials who have supported this department during the past year.
FIRE DEPARTMENT
It is with great pride that I submit my first annual report as Fire Chief for the Town of Lunenburg. I am fortunate to have had the
opportunity to work with Chief Dennis Carrier (Ret.) and am thankful for his assistance during this year of transition. The officers
and firefighters of the department deserve our endless appreciation for their dedication and outstanding service to our
community. I too, am grateful for the support they have shown me during my first year as Chief.
The single most significant event of 2002 was the voters' decision to build a new public safety complex. After nearly 20 years of
study and countless hours of discussions, we will soon break ground on a safe, state of the art facility that will house Police,
Fire, Emergency Medical Services, Communications and Emergency Management under one roof. We are extremely grateful to
the townspeople for their recognition of the need for this facility. We look forward to the building process and ultimately, moving
day.
The Fire Department was extremely fortunate to have received a federal 90% matching grant of $200,000.00. The grant was used
to purchase a forestry interface firefighting vehicle. This was a highly competitive grant, written by our own fire department
personnel. In addition we received a state grant of $21,000.00 that was used to upgrade and replace our radio equipment. These
two grants will allow us to better serve the community by upgrading our equipment while at the same time, using only a small
portion of the town's income.
The Fire Department responded to 908 calls for assistance and performed 361 inspections in 2002. In addition we spent many
hours in training for traditional firefighting techniques as well as non-traditional such as responding to terrorism events,
suspicious packages or envelopes, and the ever-expanding world of hazardous materials. Your Fire Department is trained and
prepared to assist you in anyway at anytime.

POLICE DEPARTMENT
POLICE CHIEF DANIEL F. BOURGEOIS
I would like to begin by thanking the residents of Lunenburg for their support in the passing of the Public Safety Building project
at the Town Meeting in May 2002 as well as in the ballot boxes last election. Our department was overwhelmed by the many calls
of support and time donated by residents working on our behalf in order to make this project become a reality! Your support
during these uncertain and difficult fiscal times is sincerely appreciated by all our staff and comes at a critical time in public
safety and homeland security.
A Public Safety Building Committee has been formed which includes the Chairman of the Board of Selectmen, one Planning
Board Member, three citizens-at-large, the Fire Chief and myself. The make-up of this committee offers years of experience in
public safety as well as experience in architectural design, building construction, real estate development and long range
planning. This committee is currently working diligently to design and construct a Public Safety Complex that will meet the Town
of Lunenburg's public safety needs as well as a complex we will all be proud of! This complex will house the Police Department,
Fire Department, Communications Center, Emergency Management as well as Emergency Medical Services. This complex is
scheduled to be completed by the end of summer 2004 and will be located adjacent to 635 Massachusetts Ave. (directly behind
the corner property at Beal Street and Mass Ave).
I am proud to report that Lunenburg continues to be a safe community in which to reside and visit. In review, statistics for the
year 2002 Indicate noticeable reductions in crime in the following areas; Disturbances, Assaults and Larcenies. Reductions were
also seen in violent crimes, breaks into businesses as well as domestic violence Investigations. However, calls for basic police
service and patrol Initiated incidents increased from 8,491 logged Incidents in year 2001 to 9,175 in year 2002. An additional
2,401 business and high-risk area checks were also logged. Our officers continue to dedicate themselves to protecting you and
your families from the dangers that our changing society presents. I commend both our full time and reserve officers for the
countless hours they donate free of charge in training initiatives and investigations. This dedication should reassure our
residents that we are up to the task of protecting our community from crime and disorder during these uncertain times.
Although our State funded Community Policing Grant monies have decreased by 60% over the past two years, we continue to
provide as many pro-active initiatives as possible. Initiatives such as blcycle, watercraft and walking patrols in our
neighborhoods and lake areas will continue as state funding allows. My thanks to the Hickory Hills Landowners Association and
the Lake Shirley Improvement Corp. for their assistance by directly funding additional patrols in those specific recreational areas
and neighborhoods.
Our Police Department remains pro-active within our schools offering lectures and classes in Teen Dating Violence, Bicycle
Safety, Driver Education, Stranger Danger and Drinking and Driving prevention. We continue to offer the "Are You OK" program
to our senior citizen population to reassure seniors that someone is checking on their well-being daily. We also partnered with
the Grand Lodge of Masons in the fall of 2002 and provided the Elementary School aged children with the nationally recognized
"CHIPS" (child identification) program. This initiative allowed us to fingerprint, videotape and make dental Impressions of
approximately 400 children within the school district free of charge. This information was sent home with participating students
to be kept by their family in case of an emergency.
Our police department was also successful in obtaining two additional State Grants that will provide funding for traffic safety
Initiatives as well as communications equipment. State funded grant initiatives such as the "click It or ticket" campaign allow us
to hire extra patrols designated to enforce seat belt and drunk driving laws during peak travel times and holidays. This Initiative
will continue into the summer months of 2003. The Law Enforcement Public Safety Equipment Grant initiative will allow us to
upgrade our in house and portable communications systems by purchasing new equipment.
I would like to thank the residents of Lunenburg for their patience and cooperation during major road and sewer construction projects
in 2002. As we enter another busy schedule of road projects In 2003, I anticipate further delays on major public ways such as Mass.
Ave, Prospect Street, Leominster Road as well as Electric Ave. beginning as early as mid-March. Please seek alternate routes during
construction times In these areas when possible and use caution if traveling through these construction areas.
Our department continues to actively participate in the Federal ATTF (Anti-Terrorism Task Force), State SATURN (Statewide Anti-
Terrorism Response Network) as well as the JTTF (Joint Anti-Terrorism Task Force). Our participation in these programs allows us to
keep up to date on all local and national homeland security Issues as well as training and equipment grant opportunities.
As we enter 2003 we are all faced with a struggling national and state economy, the constant threat of terrorism, the possibility
of war and an uncertain local operating budget. May I remind all that when the strength of our economy decreases the demands
on police services Increase! Historically, when the economy weakens and times get tough, police response to Incidents of
domestic violence, drug and alcohol abuse as well as larcenies and shoplifting increase! As we prepare our FY 04 budgets, we
23
===PAGE 24===
have all been asked to do more with less. The Lunenburg Police Department remains committed and dedicated to providing the
best public safety one could ask forl Our staff and I remain determined to serve the community and protect our residents in a
professional and skilled manner.

RITTER MEMORIAL LIBRARY
How You Used Your Library
You checked out 42 items every hour the Ritter Library was open,. That's 9.73 items per person per year. Of the 91,427
items checked out, 68,533 were books, 6621 were audios and 14,859 movies. Lunenburg library's check-outs ranked 4th among
the 23 Central Massachusetts libraries with comparable populations. Ritter received an average of 1,346 visits per week. We own
a collection of only 36,095 items, so there is a lot of quick turnover. The library has nine computers available for public use and
they are very much in use, especially the one public Internet PC.
Using Ritter's Catalog/Databases from Home
The catalog is online at cwmars.org and library cardholders can now place requests and access databases and
newspaper archives from home. See the database link from our homepage www.lunenburg.com/ritter. Thanks to webmaster
24
===PAGE 25===
Doug Pulsifer who has taken over for George Emond who so ably managed our website from its inception. Visit our website to
see new titles, view the extensive museum pass listing offered by the Friends, or just to find the hours.
Programming
Ritter held 114 programs for children attended by 3195 people. There were 53 teen or adult programs which drew 1722
people. The Friends of the Ritter Memorial Library and the local council of the Massachusetts Cultural Council fund nearly all
these programs, whether it is just the food or a whole performance. The Friends brought Lieber UnUsualis, a Medieval and
Renaissance music group to Lunenburg and sponsored classes on watercolor painting, genealogy, and a wonderful community
art show, just to name a few examples of their generosity.
Trustee Outreach Initiatives
Debra Chapdelaine Initiated Books on the Beach in the summer of 2002. She brought storytelling and books to children
at the Lunenburg Town Beach. Gare Thompson and Marguerite Soroka did a Book Talk & Tea at the Eagle House Senior Center
which led to Mary Ann Sudolnik's Eagle Reader Book Club. The Trustees built and raced the S.S. Minnow in Ritter's Annual
Cardboard Boat Race . all seven trustees (in full Gilliagan's Island regalia) were aboard this seaworthy vessel.
Summer Reading & More to Encourage Competent Readers
School may be out in the summer, but the Ritter Library offers families all the resources they need to keep kids
Intellectually active and reading. Studies have shown that children who participate in the educational summer programs at
libraries return to school with better skills than those who don't. Four hundred eighty seven (487) students participated in
Ritter's Summer Reading Program. They signed up and tracked their summer reading receiving incentive prizes along the way.
The Middle School React II Book Club and the High School Red Eye Book Club are still going strong. Programs for
bables, toddlers, and young readers round out the literacy initiatives sponsored by the public library to help children and their
families with the essential task of encouraging children to learn to read so they can read to learn.
State Grants Lunenburg $1,782,563 toward new library
The Massachusets Board of Library Commissioners has offered Lunenburg nearly $1.8 Million toward library construction.
The rest of the funding must be raised locally by November, 2003. The School Committee generously offered the corner field
next to Memorial Drive as a site for this new library. Best Joslin Architects has designed an extremely efficient and economical
single story facility. The Trustees are confident that the site and the economics will never be better.
Susan Tallman, Library Director Trustees: Debra Chapdelaine, Chair, Richard Letarte, Gare Thompson, Diane
Guenard, Linda Kennedy, Mary Ann Sudolnik, and Brian Laffond
HOUSING AUTHORITY
The Lunenburg Housing Authority was founded in 1968, and is charted to oversee, manage and develop State-aided housing
within the Town. Within this charter, the Authority, in participation with other community leaders, also has responsibility to
assure adequate sources of affordable private housing for Lunenburg. The Authority currently manages 48 subsidized
apartments under Chapter 667, known as Pearl Brook Housing for the Elderly. Our family housing is adjacent to the Pearl Brook
Apartments and consists of six townhouse style apartments consisting of two and three bedroom units.
Applications for State-alded housing may be obtained at 131 White Street, Lunenburg or by calling 978-345-7655 or 978-537-5300
(in Leominster). To be eligible for elderly housing, applicants must be at least 60 years old at the time of application. All other
applicants, including handicapped and disabled individuals must be of legal age to enter into a lease. The Authority will grant a
preference for Lunenburg residents. Veterans that qualify will also receive a preference. Rent is based on your household's
annual adjusted gross Income. Elderly residents will pay 30% of their adjusted Income, while residents of our family housing will
pay 25% of their adjusted Income and all utilities (excluding water and sewer).
In August of 2002, the Leominster Housing Authority began administrating the Lunenburg Housing Authority. The Lunenburg
Housing Authority believes that the management of contracting services with other agencies, in terms of quality management
services, is a valuable tool for success. This cooperative effort has allowed the Lunenburg Housing Authority to offer greater
services and assistance to our clients while maintaining our strong leadership and Involvement In the community.
The Authority has maintained its membership in the Massachusetts. Chapter of the National Association of Housing
Redevelopment Officials (NAHRO). Participation in NAHRO helps assure that the Authority is up-to-date with programs and
funding sources available for use within Lunenburg.
