PARK COMMISSION
Commissioners: Brian Kane, Cliff Worthley & Jeff Johnson . Superintendent: Tom Leppala
It is with tremendous sadness that we begin this year's town report by mentioning the passing of two of Lunenburg's finest
citizens. Dick Mulligan and Ron Lamothe were two of Lunenburg's greatest supporters of the Lunenburg Park Department and
were actively involved with the youth of this town for many, many years. These two gentlemen will be greatly missed.
In 1999 the commission developed a plan to renovate the beach house at Town Beach. Phase I Included a new roof and porch
which was completed in the spring of 2000. Phase II was completed in 2002 and included new windows, doors and some
restructuring of the Inside of the building. Completion of the facility, which will include electrical and plumbing upgrading along
with a new floor, will hopefully be completed in the spring of 2003. The Lunenburg Town Beach once again had a very
successful season in 2002 thanks to the direction of Genevieve Nass and her staff. Gen is a tremendous asset to the Park
Department and works countless hours all summer to provide a quality experience for all the patrons who frequent the beach.
The beach was once again greatly utilized by the town and the swimming instruction classes were well attended.
2002 showed a marked improvement at Wallis Park on Whalom Road. The parking was expanded and paving is expected to take
place in the spring of 2003. New fiberglass backboards were installed and new playground equipment was added. We are
hopeful to improve the lighting situation this spring as well.
This year's Summer Basketball Program headed by Bob Bourque enjoyed another successful season with a high school boys
and adult men's league program. We thank Bob for a job well done. Dick Stachowicz previously directed this program for many,
many years. He retired this past year and this commission would like to thank him for his dedication to the youth of this town
and wish him a long and enjoyable retirement. Bob was also the director of our Winter Men's League, which was made up of
predominately of Lunenburg citizens and Lunenburg graduates. We would like to thank Bob for another job well done. We
sponsor an extensive Junior Basketball Program that consists of grades 2 through 8 with a total of 40 teams and 300
participants. This includes an in-house league and a travel league program. Cliff Worthley is our Junior Basketball Director.
Cliff has been in charge of this program for the past nine years and has done an unbelievable job. Many thanks also to George
Durrance, Tom DiPerri, Chris Decot, Rick St. Jean and Sue Marabello and many others for the time and effort they have put Into
the success of this program.
Our first year with a combined DPW, consisting of the Highway, Park and Cemetery Departments, was a success. The sharing of
personnel helped us to better keep up with the huge demand on our fields and mowing during our busy seasons. As head of the
DPW we would like to thank Jack Rodriquenz for his patience and understanding during this transition.
This year we were able to add playground equipment at both Marshall Park and McNally Field. The Park Department is still
supporting the construction of a track and athletic field that will be located between the High School and Middle School. We
would like to mention here the dedication and hard work of Sandy Laserte, Steve Marsden, John lanacci, Rick Leslie and Jeff
Bingham for continuing to persevere in this endeavor.
Tom Leppala, the Park Department's one full-time employee, has once again put in a long year and has performed his duties in
exemplary fashion. Tom has done a great job for the last 19 years.
PLANNING BOARD
Marion Benson, Chair, Jerome Benson, Emerick Bakaysa, James Halloran and Deborah Seeley
The Planning Board's work has been continuous with the review and implementation of projects under the many sections of the
Zoning Bylaw, as well as the Rules and Regulations Governing the Subdivision of Land. Several projects that were approved in
2002, but, as of this writing, have not been constructed are Old Castle Precast - Leominster-Shirley Road, Twin City Baptist
addition, Omnipoint - VoiceStream Telecommunications Tower - 2005 Massachusetts Avenue, Gabes Place - 108 Reservoir
Road, and J. Glenn's Auto Salon addition - 332 Massachusetts Avenue. New Outlooks - Massachusetts Avenue and Proctor
Cash and Carry - Electric Avenue have been completed.
Projects in review or construction progress are AT &T Wireless 2005 Massachusetts Avenue, Keyspan - Leominster-Shirley
Road, Maplewood Estates - Old Farm Road, Robbs Terrace, Shaker Court Burrage Street, St Boniface - 795 Massachusetts
Avenue, Sky Cycle - 321 Electric Avenue, The Woodery - 194 Pleasant Street, Village at Flat Hill - Flat Hill Road, Whites Woods
Massachusetts Avenue, Windermere Drive Chase Road, North Middlesex Bank - Massachusetts Avenue, Lunenburg Track and
Athletic Field, Teen Center - Memorial Drive, Robbs Terrace - off Robbs Hill Road and Whites Woods Phase II. Projects in the
26
===PAGE 27===
conceptual stage or with incomplete submittals are Stone Farm . 792 Massachusetts Avenue, XQS Corporation - Chase Road,
Harley House . 909 Massachusetts Avenue and Goodrich Woods Goodrich Street. A 40B project at 1229 Massachusetts
Avenue will be forthcoming. Several other small 40B projects have been noted to the Planning Board.
The Board continues to work on siting of telecommunication towers within Town owned property and remains very cognizant of
disturbance of siting of towers in residential areas.
The Planning Board works with the above projects under the Development Plan Review process, Special Permitting Authority via
Bylaws and the Rules and Regulations Governing the Subdivision of Land.
Two major Town projects prioritized by the Master Plan and voted by the citizens are the new Primary School at 1401
Massachusetts Avenue and the proposed Public Safety Building at 635 Massachusetts Avenue. The Primary School has gone
through the Development Plan Review process and was approved by the Board. The proposed Public Safety Building is in the
Building Committee planning process. Members of the Board are representative on these committee projects.
The Board has completed a re-write of the Rules and Regulations through the Statutory Requirement this year. This re-write
brings the document up to date statutorily and encompasses the latest engineering techniques and processes.
The Annual 2002 Town Meeting passed the following new bylaws as presented by the Planning Board. These bylaws are entitled
Demolition Delay, Car Washes, Kennels, Inns and a Whalom Overlay District. In addition, there was a Zoning Map amendment
presented by petition on Leominster-Shirley Road.
The Board has presented the application to the State for Executive Order 418 which permits grant monies for planning of four
elements; housing, economic development, transportation and open space/resource protection. A balance of housing diversity
is still difficult to Implement in Lunenburg. The EO418 planning tools will assist in promoting housing across a broad range of
Income levels. Lunenburg needs economic stability. Planning within the premise of EO418 will place emphasis on sewer needs
for industries as well as plan for a variety of economic development options. Transportation is viewed as a regional element.
The impact of residential, commercial and Industrial growth within and outside our Town needs an assessment of present
conditions and Identification of problems. This work will be done in conjunction with the Montachusett Regional Planning
Commission. Lastly, the open space/resource element will continue to plan in the direction this community has taken with
emphasis on protection of water and wetlands. The culmination of this work, with other Boards, will be Integrated in our Master
Plan, or, co-currently known as the Community Development Plan.
The Board is working within the Envision of 2007 and reviewing the need for re-design of the Town Center necessitated by the
vacancies by proposed new School and Public Safety buildings. The Planning Board will work with the Board of Selectmen and
other Boards to develop a plan to re-create the center to reflect the essence of Lunenburg.
The Board continuously works toward policy writing to insure good land use criteria, and will also continue to work with all land
use boards. The members serve on other committees; the Montachusett Regional Planning Commission, Montachusett Joint
Transportation Commission, Capital Planning, School Building, Public Safety Building and CAFO Search Committees.
The Board foresees the continued need for diligent attention to Town Planning and active Board participation in all aspects of
planning and development.
TECHNOLOGY DEPARTMENT
Derek Dufresne, Technology Director
The technology department has many concerns centrally located around funding of the technology within all town departments
including the schools. I believe that one-word sums up the last two years. That word is Floccinaucinihiipilification.
Floccinaucinihililification, which means H the estimation of something as valueless", has occurred all too often. If technology
has not been categorized as such, then funds should correspondingly go up. They have dropped significantly, and will bottom
out in FY '04. Please remember Floccinaucinillplificatio
The results of such happen immediately. Delays, inoperable computers out of warranty, viruses, printers, and lack of training
are the results. A technology support team of two, who do solve the problems, are very stretched. Nerves of the users
stretched. Worst of all, faith in the equipment lost.
27
===PAGE 28===
The Federal Technology Budget percentage is 3% of the overall budget. Comparisons of Massachusetts school districts has an
average of 4% of the overall school budget going toward technology. Lunenburg is far from that at the town level (excluding the
schools) Is 2%, and the school is at just less than 1% of the overall school budget. FY '04 is very bleak.
Technology must be kept up with. The Needs Analysis completed yearly should be adhered to. The budget needs to have
dollars for software and licensing. Staff needs to be trained, and support staff reinforced. I understand about the budget
problems, but technology is the tool used everyday. If we could go back to paper and pencil, we would need to hire at least two
people extra per department, or 24 positions, at an average of $20k per year is $480,000 per year for administration (not including
taxes and Insurance)!I The funding at the end of the needs analysis was $460,000 including funding for all instructional
technology (computer labs, student computers, etc..). That is a savings of at least $20,000 just in the administration. I have tried
many ways to convey the needs for technology in the town. The thought process has to change In technology from crisis
handling to preventative maintenance. The data and function is just too valuable.
At this time, I would like to give two thumbs up to: Beth Caron (keeping me sane.), High School Interns, The Snowplows.
275TH ANNIVERSARY COMMITTEE
The 275th Anniversary Committee was established by vote of the 1999 annual town meeting. The purpose of the committee is to
plan the observance of Lunenburg's 275th Anniversary of Incorporation as a Town, which will occur in the year 2003.
Jean Hobbs is Chairperson and Donald Gillis Is Vice Chair, with Helen Brockelman, Secretary and Charles Deming, Sr.,
Treasurer. Other members are Mary Bingham, Robert H. Bowen, Kathleen Comeau, Edward Dextraze, Peter Lincoln, Ray
MacCracken, and William Proctor, Sr. Dean DeFrancesco serves as volunteer consultant to the committee for the staging and
lighting of events.
Repairs and restoration of the clock in the belfry of the Town Hall are completed, and lights Installed by volunteer Wayne
Comeau. The bell in the tower tolled at midnight on December 31st to officially open the 275th Celebration and is now chiming out
the hours. The 275th Committee wants to thank the donors to the clock fund; all contributors are listed on a plaque in the
entryway of the Town Hall.
Ed Dextraze and Pete Lincoln coordinated Lunenburg First Knight much to the delight of children and grownups alike. This was
also a "first" to be televised over local access channels viewed in Lunenburg, Fitchburg and Leominster. Volunteers from the
local channels assisted with the programming.
The 275th fundraising sub-committee headed by Bill Proctor is now soliciting sponsors for bands to march in the 275th parade on
Sunday, September 7th. Anyone Interested in being a sponsor should contact Mr. Proctor. Organizations or groups interested in
entering a float or other vehicles can contact Kathy Comeau, Parade Committee Chairperson, or any member of the parade
committee; Stanley Barney, Richard Bishop, Kathy Dupuis, Pete Lincoln, Ray MacCracken, Joel Monaco, and Joann O'Sullivan.
A yearlong celebration is planned for 2003. A Valentine Dance was held in February, coordinated by the Proctor Family Group.
On Sunday, May 25th, townspeople can join in a Memorial Day program in Veterans' Park and a Pancake Breakfast at the Eagle
House. Town Band concerts begin at the lower common gazebo in June, with 275th commemorative items for sale. Featured for
Thursday, July 24th is a "Bring Your Own Picnic on the Green" at the lower common starting at 5:00 PM. A performance by the
Yankee Brass Band follows at 7:00 PM. Beginning the last week in July a Hospitality House will be manned at the Eagle House to
provide information on times and locations of activities with tickets to events and commemorative items available.
Old Home Days, with a variety of activities, begins with Incorporation Day on the High School grounds on the evening of Friday,
August 1st, This is the actual date of the incorporation of our town in 1728. Included are a block dance, entertainment and
refreshments by the Music Alders, with a gigantic fireworks display topping the festivities.
Saturday, August 2nd is Tour Day, with both trolley and self-guided tours of the town center. The Lunenburg Historical Society
will be open to the public, with an antique tractor and other machines demonstrated by John Dupuis. The Town Hall will also be
open to the public, featuring a Civil War display and Michael Daly, Civil War re-enactor. Other happenings are a Children's
Parade of decorated carriages, bikes and wagons and other children's activities, coordinated by the PTO and School Community
Links, plus a Crafter's Marketplace, arranged by Michelle Banton and Kimberly Massak. In addition, there will be an Art Exhibit
and sale featuring local and area artists organized by Karen Kemp and Ena Thompson and a box luncheon available from the St.
Boniface Woman's Group. As a crowning touch, an auction will be held for a painting of Lunenburg center by former resident
and well-known artist Donald Demers .

The Lunenburg High School Alumnl Association will hold their annual reunion organized by Alumni Secretary Marion Walsh in
the evening.
Sunday, August 3rd will feature a performance of combined church choirs under the direction of Donald Adams, and an afternoon
tea at the Harley House, sponsored by the Lunenburg Woman's Club. Ritter Library staff will lead a "Ritter Day for Children" on

ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
The Zoning Board of Appeals heard many petitions during calendar year 2002. There were several applicants seeking variances
special permits and findings. The Zoning Board meetings are held on the second and fourth Wednesdays of the month on an
"as needed" basis.
The Zoning Board consists of the following members: Donald F. Bowen, Chairman, Samuel J. Costa, John S. Czarnecki, Alfred J.
Gravelle and Damien Riddle. Associate members are James Besarkarski and Raymond Beal.
The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statutes are as follows:
1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from the Town's Protective By- Laws, as
authorized in the By-laws.
2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town's Protective By-
laws.
3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts
General Laws, as amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain
a permit or enforcement action from the administrative officer or Board in the Town, the
Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or other administrative official.
4. To act upon applications for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General
Laws, Chapter 40B. The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits
submitted to the board under Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing.
These requests can, by law, circumvent local zoning ordinances.
If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please call (978) 582-4146, Monday through Thursday 8:00 AM
3:30 PM. Application for Zoning Board filings may be obtained in the office of the Town Clerk during regular office hours. The
filing process may seem daunting and time consuming but we are here to help. Lisa Normandin, Administrative Assistant, is
available for questions regarding the forms or with any other questions you may have.

MONTACHUSETT REGIONAL VOCATIONAL TECHNICAL SCHOOL (MONTY TECH)
The Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical District School Committee presents this report to the citizens of the
District. The Committee consists of twenty-two (22) members four from Fitchburg represented by Sunnie Epstein, James
O'Donnell, John E. Murray, Jr., and LeRoy Clark, two from Gardner represented by Charles R. LeBlanc and Helen Lepkowski and
one from each of the communities of Ashburnham represented by Diane Swenson, Ashby - represented by Warren Landry,
Athol- represented by Toni L. Phillips, Barre - represented by Sarah Dyer, Harvard - represented by Dr. Henry Lefkovits, Holden
- represented by Nancy Anderson, Hubbardston - represented by Jacqueline B. Kaminski, Lunenburg - - represented by Dr.
Charles F. Valera, Petersham - represented by Ed Simms, Phillipston - represented by Eric Olson, Princeton - represented by
John Mollica, Royalston - represented by Mary C. Barclay, Sterling - represented by Dr. Kenneth I.H. Williams, Templeton -
represented by David Roche, Westminster - represented by Jeffrey Schutt and Winchendon - represented by Burton Gould, Jr.
At the organizational meeting held in June, Dr. Charles Valera of Lunenburg, was elected Chairperson. Ed Simms of
Petersham was elected Vice-President, Norman LeBlanc was elected Treasurer, and David LaPierre, Superintendent-Director,
was elected District Clerk. Monty Tech's School Council has continued to provide guidance and direction to the administration
in its efforts to continually improve the educational experience of students attending Monty Tech.
FINANCIAL REPORT
Preparing and development of the fiscal year 2003 budget was a difficult task, considering the state's financial crisis as
well as the Impact it was having on our member communities. With great cooperation, our Educational Plan was passed by all
member communities. This plan reflected a modest 3.9% increase over fiscal year 2002. Actual Chapter 70 state aid was less
than anticipated and required a reduction of $230,000 In our fiscal year 2003 budget. The final Educational Plan, totaling
$15,148,287, reflects a 2.5% increase over fiscal year 2002. The yearly audit of the District's books was completed in August by
the accounting firm of Melanson & Heath from Greenfield, Massachusetts. An excellent report was received without findings or
issues.
STUDENT BODY
Monty Tech's enrollment as of October 1st Included 1,139 students in grades nine through twelve. Students were
representative of all eighteen (18) communities in the district. Ashburnham (51), Ashby (45), Athol (103), Barre (14), Fitchburg
(326), Gardner (127), Harvard (1), Holden (38), Hubbardston (35), Lunenburg (40), Petersham (9), Phillipston (16), Princeton (23),
Royalston (19), Sterling (51), Templeton (50), Westminster (56) and Winchendon (91). In addition, there are 44 out-of-district
students attending.
The Guidance Department/Admissions Department processed 572 applications for admission in September 2002. Of these, the
majority were for the 325 available ninth grade openings. This was one of the highest numbers of applications in recent history.
Transfer opportunities for students in grades ten and eleven continue to be offered where space is available.
Monty Tech continued to offer numerous opportunities for students, parents and community members to become
knowledgeable about and visit the school. In October, over seventeen hundred district eighth graders participated in Tour Days.
Students toured our twenty-one vocational/technical areas and learned about the challenging academic offerings and exciting
athletic and extracurricular programs. This was followed by Career Awareness Night. This Open House, as well as the spring
Open House, was attended by hundreds of students, parents and community members from throughout the district. The
Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P.) continued to attract large numbers of seventh and eighth graders during two separate five-
week winter sessions. Participants had opportunities to attend hands-on after school sessions in a variety of
vocational/technical areas.
The J.R.O.T.C. Program had a successful first year. First year cadets showed their skills and our school by
participating in numerous flag ceremonies. The drill team earned recognition in both state and regional competitions. Select
cadets attended and enjoyed mock boot camp at Camp Quantico in Virginia. Student interest in this program has grown
considerably throughout the year.
In June, the Class of 2002 graduated 216 seniors. These graduates received diplomas and technical certificates.
Approximately 95% of the graduates planned to work or continue their education at the post-secondary level, while another 5%
planned to enter the military service. The Tech Prep Articulation Agreements with local colleges continued to play an important
role in helping our graduates enter college with credits earned at Monty Tech. Nearly half of our graduates entered a two or four
year college. This number has grown nearly every year.
The Scholarship Committee awarded approximately $18,000 in scholarships to members of the Class of 2002. Once
again, local (Business Education Enrichment Foundation) and state organizations, as well as generous Individuals, continue to
recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech graduates in the form of grants and scholarships. The School Committee,
administration, faculty and graduates themselves are grateful for this support.
FACULTY AND STAFF
In April of 2002, Montachusett Regional was selected as one of four vocational/technical schools In the state to
participate in the EMMS (Exemplary Models for School Success) Grant. This competitive grant, which was one of five national
grants to be awarded, involves the development of a replicable model reflecting the key practices of High Schools That Work
(HSTW), a nationally recognized model for improvement of academic and technical student achievement. To this end, a School
Improvement Team was established and has been actively engaged in the process of positive school change. The School
Improvement Team (SIT) attended the HSTW Conference in July and has also participated five full-day workshops since that
30
===PAGE 31===
time. An eleven-member HSTW site team visited Monty Tech on October 21, 22 and 23rd and collaborated in setting school goals
and current performance levels.
Currently the staff includes fifty-four (54) vocational teachers and forty-two (42) academic teachers, six (6) special
needs teachers, one (1) vocational integration teacher, one (1) vocational specialist, four (4) guidance counselors, one (1) school
adjustment counselor, one (1) school social worker, two (2) deans of students, (1.5) school nurses, one (1) part-time
psychologist, one (1) part-time speech and language therapist, one (1) vocational paraprofessional and four (4) special needs
aides. Four (4) teachers retired since last January including an Information Technology instructor, an Electronics instructor, an
English teacher and a Machine Technology instructor. The School Committee welcomed new faculty members and staff In
Masonry/Tech Ed, Social Studies, Automotive Technology, Special Needs, Math, Media Center and Technology.
Vocational/technical services were enhanced with the addition of a vocational integration and vocational/sped Instructor. Plans
are currently underway for administrative changes including the position of Assistant Director for Grants and Accountability and
the filling of the Assistant Director/Principal position.
CURRICULUM
As the time for graduation draws closer, the class of 2003, the first class mandated to pass MCAS, has again made
considerable Improvement. Monty Tech has exceeded its target score in both English/LA and Mathematics for the Cycle II
reporting period. In the English Language Arts, 63% of the students tested passed, compared with 57% in 2002 and 31% in 2001.
In Mathematics, 53% of the students tested passed, compared with 35% and 17% in previous years. Numerous efforts to
improve our student's MCAS scores continued this year. Test taking strategies with other Interventions, Including after school
and vacation programs in English Language Arts and Math, were implemented. This fall, a semester course in Study Skills was
implemented for all grade 9 and 10 students. There were several curriculum changes and initiatives Implemented or continued
during the school year. These included: MCAS Summer Camp, Stop and Read, Summer Reading List, Curriculum
Review/Revision, Test and Data Analysis and Vocational/Technical Alignment. Data analysis of MCAS testing data was done and
remedial programs in English Language Arts and Math has begun for students who have not yet achieved a score of 220.
Several factors have been researched including reading level, courses taken and attendance. A correlation has been made
between poor attendance and poor performance on MCAS and an Attendance Improvement Plan is being developed.
The Vocational-Technical Programs continued their tradition of house building with the completion of their 26th house-
building project which was a ranch style home In Westminster. The project for the 2002-2003 school year is one of the bigger
projects we have undertaken. The construction cluster students are constructing three (3) affordable homes in Gardner in
conjunction with the Gardner Community Development Council. In addition to the house-building project the
vocational/technical areas continue to provide communities in the Monty Tech district with a number of services as a component
of their individual curriculums. The projects listed below are but a few of those accepted this year.

SPECIAL SERVICES
The Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District currently provides special education services for two
hundred and sixty-seven students. Our mission Is to comply with Public Law 105-17, the 1997 amendments to the Individuals
with Disabilities Act (known as IDEA-97") and Chapter 766 of the Acts of 1972, MGL c.71B (603CMR 28.00) to provide a free
appropriate public education (FAPE) to students with disabilities in the least restrictive environment.
Students are Identified, evaluated, and, when necessary, special education services are prescribed to help them
progress effectively in regular and special education classes to develop their individual educational potential. All students'
educational plans are developed by the prescribed Team with parental involvement and Individualized to meet their needs.
Parents are invited to participate in initial, review, or re-evaluation Team meetings and are notified in their primary language. Our
Parents' Rights Pamphlets are in English and Spanish. Physical and occupational therapy services are provided on an as-
needed basis (speech therapy is provided to forty students per week).
We have created the position of Special Education Vocational Resource Teacher, who works with the special needs
students in their trades to assure that the trade is appropriate to the students' capabilities and aspirations and they will achieve
to their highest potential. This was recommended by the Department of Education during their Spring 2002 audit.
The Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District Program for the Deaf continues to provide a
comprehensive vocational and academic educational program for qualified students Identified as being sensory impaired in the
area of hearing. Presently, there are six students from the district being served by one certified teacher of the deaf, with three
full-time interpreter assistants and two part-time Interpreter assistants and a speech pathologist. These students are In regular
education classes with assistance from Interpreters. The climate and culture of the Monty Tech environment have made for a
very successful Integration of the hearing impaired students.
32
===PAGE 33===
STUDENT AFFAIRS AND ATHLETICS
The National Honor Society's winter induction ceremony honored nine new members of which six were juniors and
three were seniors for outstanding leadership, character, scholarship and service.
Monty Tech continues to be a one hundred (100%) percent Skills USA-VICA school. Local competitions saw eight
hundred plus students test their skills against industry-validated standards. One hundred and twenty (120) went on to compete
at the state level, earning numerous medals including a Gold Medal in Commercial Design. We also saw our first statewide
officer elected from Monty Tech this year. Monty Tech will host the State Competitions in May of 2003.
The USA First Robotics Program has completed its second year and continues to grow. All member district high
schools were invited to joint Monty Tech and its business partners for year two. Fitchburg High School and Oakmont Regional
parents, advisors and students, together with Monty Tech's advisors and students, worked with our corporate partners at
Tyco/Simplex/Grinnel to engineer, design, build and ship their robot In just six weeks. The team competed In the New England
Regionals in New Haven, CT where we finished seventeenth in a field of sixty-seven teams.
The Bulldog Pride Program is In full swing. This program is a positive motivational pride-building program. Prizes are
awarded at four quarterly drawings and Include such items as mountain bikes, VCRs and radios. The end-of-the year grand prize
was one (1) refurbished car.
The athletic teams had a very successful 2001-2002 school year. Over three hundred and fifty (350) students were
involved in one or more of our athletic programs. Freshman Girls Basketball was necessitated by the large number of freshman
girls applying for the sport.
Last fall, the Football team finished at 3 and 8, but played tough all year. The Freshmen team won 5 games and lost
only 1. The Varsity Boys Soccer team was 13 7 1, which qualified them for the District Tournament and the State Vocational
Tournament, where they were the runners-up. The JV Boys Soccer team finished at 6 and 4. The Field Hockey team was 2- 15-
1, as the younger girls are trying to learn the skills needed to compete at a higher level. The Girls Volleyball team finished 11 6
and qualified for the District Tournament in their first year of existence. The Varsity Girls Soccer team had a record of 5 - 10 3,
as they were rebuilding the program. The Co-Ed Cross Country team won 5 and lost 11. The Golf team finished at 7- 7 and
qualified for the District Tournament as a team for only the second time in 28 years. The Varsity Cheerleaders competed in the
first Colonial Athletic League Cheerleading competition and finished third.
The winter season saw our Girls Basketball team reel off 23 straight wins before losing to Bromfield in the District semi-
finals. They won the Colonial Athletic League title and the State Vocational Tournament. This was done with a total of eight (8)
players on the varsity. The Boys Basketball team also qualified for the post-season tournament with an 11 9 record. They beat
a tough Maynard team in the first round before being defeated by a strong Worcester Voc. team. The JV Boys Basketball team
won 15 games and lost only 5 while the Freshmen went 2 and 16. The Wrestling team was 2 - 10, but the young wrestlers are
due to pin a few opponents this year. Our winter Cheerleaders went to the Colonial Athletic League winter competition and
finished fourth. The Co-op Ice Hockey team ran into some tough times, as they only won two or three games all season. The
numbers were up for underclass participation, which should lead to better success this year.
Finally, the spring season recap - The Varsity Softball team had an outstanding year. Despite losing 9 starting
positions due to graduation, the team won 17 games and lost only 5. The team was far better than anyone thought it would be,
including Head Coach Dave Reid. They won the Colonial Athletic League title for the fifth straight year and played in the State
Vocational Tournament for the third straight year. The JV Softball team went 7 7, with some of these girls moving up to help
the Varsity this year. Varsity Baseball qualified for the District Tournament with a 12 and 6 record. They lost to Auburn 9 to 6 in
the first round. The JV Baseball team went 7 - 8 and with 16 freshmen on the team, the future should be bright. Boys Track &
Field had a 1 - 6 record, while Girls Track & Field was 2 - 8. The first year Boys Volleyball team went 5 and 9 in varsity
competition.

TECHNOLOGY
Monty Tech continues to maintain a student to computer ratio that is above both the state average and Massachusetts
Department of Education Local Technology Plan Benchmark Standards. As our network continues to grow, traffic patterns,
33
===PAGE 34===
overall performance and network security are issues which are currently being addressed. We are now in the process of
converting to fiber optic connections in a number of high concentration technology areas. The wireless laptop lab in the Media
Center has proven to be very effective as a teaching tool. While some modifications remain to be completed in the TV Studio,
the studio itself is now fully operational. Manned by a volunteer student crew, morning announcements are broadcast daily.
Volcemail and e-mail are becoming the preferred method of staff communication throughout the building. Last year, the
Information Technology Program revamped its support of the IT Help Desk. The success of the program exceeded everyone's
expectations. Virtually all computer problems are addressed within 4 hours of being reported, with the vast majority being
tested and repaired by students from the Information Technology area.
GRANTS AND CONTRACTS
Monty Tech continues to aggressively pursue grant funding to assist the school in providing a comprehensive array of
educational and social services for its students. Money from a variety of state and federal sources totaled $973,199 for fiscal
year 2002. These state and federal sources Include: Academic Support Services, Class Size Reduction, Elsenhower
Professional Development, High Schools That Work, Health Protection, PALMS: Professional Development, Perkins: Secondary
Occupational Education, Perkins: New Technology; Perkins: Tech Prep, Safe and Drug Free Schools, Safe Schools for
Gay/Lesblan Students, Special Education, Special Education: Professional Development, Title I and Title IV.
CONCLUDING STATEMENT
The School Committee, administration, faculty and staff are proud of the accomplishments achieved by the students
who choose to pursue a vocational/technical education at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School. Their continued
contribution to the economic stability and workforce needs of our member communities provides evidence of the quality
education at Monty Tech. At this time, it is clear that this time of high stakes testing and academic accountability requires a
delicate balance between increased academic rigor and preserving the quality and integrity of our students' technical programs.
Monty Tech will continue to work with the business community, local educational institutions, higher education, and its
vocational technical counterparts across the Commonwealth to develop common sense plans and resources to the educational
challenges we face. Monty Tech is committed to continued educational improvement and will provide comprehensive
educational opportunities designed to equip our students with the skills and knowledge necessary to be productive members of
our society and life long learners and to that end, respectfully invites and encourages the continued support of town officials,
parents, and citizens in each of its member communities.

SUPERINTENDENT'S MESSAGE
Loxi Jo Calmes, Acting Superintendent
By action of the School Committee on November 6, 2002 the Lunenburg Public Schools adopted a five-year strategic plan that
will guide the operation of the schools. While always important, in times of fiscal emergencies like the one the Commonwealth
and by extension the Town and the District are experiencing, the strategic plan provides a validated format for decision making
and resource allocation. The group that developed the plan represented a cross section of the community, which Included
students, parents, faculty, administrators, a member of the school committee and community leaders. Serving on this
committee were: Christie Letarte, Keith Hochstein, Susan Cavaioli, Carol Higgins, Anna Keegan, Mary Ann Weisman, Chad
Adams, Milree Keeling, Elaine Murphy, Roger St. Jean, Cynthia Daukantas, Colleen Stillman, Michael Barney and Loxl Jo Calmes.
Our Vision: The Lunenburg Public Schools prides itself on excellence. We provide a child-centered environment that challenges
all learners to achieve their highest potential. This is accomplished through a partnership with a supportive community.
Our Mission: We prepare our students for life-long learning and responsible community membership.
Our Guiding Beliefs-We Belleve:
Education is a community partnership.
Learning is reflective, creative, ongoing and takes place in all environments.
A successful school actively engages students and staff.
All students have talents and can excel.
Professional growth is fundamental to instructional excellence.
All students are capable of learning and are entitled to instructional excellence.
Staff and students are entitled to a safe, healthy and positive learning environment.
An atmosphere of mutual respect is essential for learning.
Our Goals and Objectives:
1. To foster the talents, skills, and potential of all students.
1.1. To expose all students to a variety of opportunities to explore individual interests and talents.
1.2. To develop a process and structure for implementing transition plans for each student.
2. To ensure that staff and programs are conducive to instructional excellence.
2.1. To develop and Implement a process of data-drive decision-making.
2.2. To create a professional development program based on data and need.
2.3. To identify staffing needs and make recommendations for hiring staff.
3. To use technology as an integral component of teaching, learning, and management.
3.1. To develop a district technology plan to address equipment and staffing needs.
3.2. To provide professional development to raise the level of appropriate technological use in the instructional program
and to Improve communication.
4. To provide facilities that meet staff, program, and community needs.
4.1. To complete a needs assessment for building transitions related to construction of the new primary school.
4.2. To develop and implement a centralized process for building use.
4.3. To work in partnership with the community to promote present and future capital projects.
35
===PAGE 36===
After a five-week summer program that provided extended learning opportunities to students PK-11, our schools opened at the
end of August, but without some long-time, well-known and respected staff members. While retirees Margaret Curley, Peter
Lincoln, Richard Stachowicz, Tom Starr, and Joyce Wass will be missed, our administrative team began their quest to find new
staff early and once again this year our good fortune prevailed with the hiring of some first-rate people in a variety of positions.
The School Committee also welcomed a new member. Dr. David Reif came on board in May replacing veteran member and
former Chair, Ernest Hunter.
We are very excited as the Primary Building Project goes out for bld and moves into the construction phase. Construction is
targeted to begin in April 2003 and will take 17-18 months to complete. In times of challenge this building project represents our
confidence in our town, our state and our nation. We are building for the future, and we are thankful for the community leaders
that worked to make this vision a reality.
The "No Child Left Behind" Act Influenced much of our district and Improvement work throughout the year. This Act is the
reauthorization of the federal Elementary and Secondary Schools Act. The intent of the legislation is to ensure every child in the
nation receive a quality education. High academic proficiency levels, highly qualified teachers, a safe, drug-free and conducive
learning environment, and all students graduating from high school are the goals of the legislation. Our schools are working with
the Massachusetts Department of Education on a Consolidated District Planning Initiative. Starting last spring the NCLB Team
began the district planning process. Student data has been reviewed, performance gaps Identified, and Improvement objectives
are in the process of being developed. The next steps include identifying research-based strategies to address the improvement
objectives and developing a three-year strategic plan. This collaborative, data-driven, and research-based process will ensure
students and teachers receive the needed resources to address our Improvement objectives.
The fifth administration of the Massachusetts Comprehensive Assessment System (MCAS) took place in the spring of 2002. In
Lunenburg 96% of tenth grade students met the required benchmark for graduation in English/Language Arts and 90% met the
required benchmark for graduation in Mathematics. Overall, eighty-seven percent (87%) of tenth grade students passed both
required tests. Forty students (31%) met the performance criteria to apply for the Stanley Z. Koplik Certificate of Mastery.
Currently, ALL students attending Lunenburg High School have met the MCAS graduation requirement and upon completion of
the local graduation requirements will leave with a diploma.
MCAS performance Is reported by level (Advanced, Proficient, Needs Improvement, and Warning). Students at the Advanced
Level demonstrate a comprehensive and In-depth understanding of rigorous subject matter and provide sophisticated solutions
to complex problems. Students at the Proficient Level demonstrate a solid understanding of challenging subject matter and
solve a wide variety of problems. Students at the Needs Improvement Level demonstrate partial understanding of subject matter
and solve some simple problems. Students at the Warning Level demonstrate minimal understanding of subject matter and do
not solve even simple problems. In grade 3 results are reported in only three categories, Proficient, Needs Improvement, and
Warning.

HIGH SCHOOL
Lunenburg High School is pleased to announce that all members of the Class of 2003 passed the MCAS in both English
Language Arts and Mathematics. We are proud of all our students and faculty for meeting the challenge in the first year of state
competency determination for the issuance of a diploma. LHS also posted a performance rating of "High" in both English
Language Arts and Mathematics this year from the Massachusetts Department of Education, showing an improvement rating of
"Above Target" in both areas for Cycle II.
It has been an exciting year for many other reasons. We have resurrected both the Multicultural Festival and the School Musical
("Grease") this year, as well as carrying on traditions such as Homecoming and the 38th annual Competitive Class Plays. Our
students have traveled both domestically and abroad, to Florida, France and Italy. Our athletic teams have performed admirably,
with our football team sharing the league title, girl's cross country going 7-1, our boys track team winning the league and District
E championship, our boy's basketball team being named as the sportsmanship award winners, our hockey team achieving
league co-champ status, the girl's soccer teams qualifying for district play as well as winning the league sportsmanship award,
and our girl's basketball team winning the Mid-Wachusett C championship as well as the Mount Wachusett Invitational
Basketball Tournament.
Our music students participated in the Massachusetts Instrumental and Choral Conductors Association Festival for high school
ensembles, as well as Band Day at UMASS. Our construction class completed the restoration of the spire for the town hall. We
instituted a new applied graphic design course in the art department. As you can tell, our students are always doing something
new and exciting at LHS, and are fine representatives of our school and community.
We are looking forward to the implementation of a revised schedule next year to allow for the creation of the STEP program
(Senior Transitional Experience Program), a community based elective program for seniors involving an internship, community
service or college experience. We look forward to the development of this program as a way to meet the needs of all our
students and to foster our district's mission of preparing our students for life-long learning and responsible community
membership.
As always, we thank the community of Lunenburg for its continued enthusiastic support. We welcome your Involvement In our
school community through event attendance, volunteering and supporting our students in any way you can. This positive
partnership helps us continue to provide an excellent education for our greatest resource, our youth.

TURKEY HILL MIDDLE SCHOOL
During the 2002-2003 school year, Turkey Hill Middle School continued in a variety of ways to pursue excellence. The faculty
and administration view this pursuit not as a static accomplishment but rather a fluid process. As such, significant gains were
made in some areas and lesser gains in other areas, but everyone at THMS seeks excellence and continual progress.
This past year the staff at THMS engaged in meaningful, rewarding and profitable faculty meetings that revolved around the
"Results" philosophy. There are three faculty meetings per month. Teachers met with colleagues from the same subject at one
of these meetings per month as well as on our half-day workshops to discuss curriculum and Instruction. First they scrutinized
the MCAS Item Analysis to ascertain our students' weak skill areas. Next they shared common teaching strategies to determine
the best technique for teaching those skills. They established obtainable goals for students to accomplish and then taught or re-
taught the skill using the agreed upon strategy. The following month at the next "Results" meeting they revisited their
accomplishments as well as areas that needed further refinement. Teachers greeted this process with excitement. It provided
ongoing professional development focused on data and results. The "Results" approach provided teachers with the opportunity
to share successful Instructional practices, to dialogue about the art of teaching and to discuss areas of concern.
40
===PAGE 41===
This process as well as our subject area task forces continued our focus to promote consistency and standardization among
teachers. Curriculum mapping is the name of this effort, and the goal is to ensure that all children receive an educational
program that has the same high quality standards. This is an ongoing process. Each year teachers make adjustments in their
program to match the Standards of the Massachusetts Frameworks. This year THMS met the performance targets in all areas as
established by the Department of Education. As our school moves into Cycle 3 of this program, our goal will always be to meet
or exceed target goals.
Turkey Hill Middle School took a big step forward in another area. For the first time our students had access to a computer lab.
Twenty-five computers were installed in the library, and all were connected to the Internet. This was long overdue. Technology
education remains a high priority for all students, and we cannot allow this school to lag behind. With the addition of this lab, all
teachers can take an entire class to the library to do research, to create Power Point presentations, to design science labs and
do a variety of other activities. The effort to improve the physical plant continued. A community school partnership again used
funds ralsed by the school to remodel an area of the school; this year we chose to renovate the front office counter, doors and
trim. There is now a brand new look to this important focal point of the school. Two years ago the cafeteria was renovated, last
year the front courtyard was re-landscaped and now the front office is revitalized. It is all the result of terrific school-community
partnerships, and it was all done no cost to the town!
THMS continues to improve our ability to meet the needs of all students. This year the Guidance Team created a system which
increases the level of communication among teachers. Designated as the Student Data Sharing System, the system ensures that
information about a student is shared with all of the student's teachers. If a student is having an academic or social problem, the
system begins to accumulate information and Initiates a sharing system so that all teachers are aware of the problem. Lastly we
have Initiated a new tradition here at THMS. Our eighth grade class will travel to Washington DC in June. This class trip serves
many functions. It reinforces lessons learned in social studies about the history and geography of Washington; it provides
students first hand examples of lessons learned in civics; and it gives our graduating class a chance to make friendships, to
have memorable experiences and to Increase the positive feelings they have about their middle school experience.

THOMAS C. PASSIOS ELMENTARY SCHOOL
The Thomas C. Passios Elementary School continues to strive toward educating the total child. We utilize a variety of
instructional methods and modalities in our approach to meet the individual needs of every child. We believe that the success of
our programs is dependent upon a healthy and productive partnership between home and school. Toward that end, parents play
an active role in many of the daily programs and routines of their child's educational program.
This year continues to be a challenging and exciting year for the students, faculty, and staff of the Elementary and Primary
Schools. With a combined enrollment of seven hundred students, class sizes continue in the twenty to twenty-four range, with
the younger children benefiting from smaller class sizes.
The summer of 2002 proved to be very beneficial to many children in Lunenburg. Again we were able to offer a number of
extended learning opportunities to children: a five week program for children with special needs in grades preschool through
grade 4, and a five week program for children in Grades 1-4 who were experiencing difficulty in the areas of reading and math.
Funding for the Grades 1 and 2 programs was provided through School Linked Services; State Academic Support grant monies
supported the Grade 3 and 4 program.
As the Elementary School continues to analyze our students' results on the Massachusetts Comprehensive Assessment System
(MCAS) and revise curricula and instructional strategies, we continue to grow as an excellent educational facility. We are
extremely proud of the work the faculty continues to undertake each year. As teachers reflect on their teaching practice,
students benefit.
We are pleased to report that the Thomas C. Passios Elementary School met its targeted Improvement goal as determined by the
Department of Education. Results on the State Grade 4 MCAS Tests indicate that in the areas of both English/Language Arts and
Math, the percentage of Grade 4 students performing in the "failing" category continues to decline, while those performing In the
proficient and advanced categories increases.
This year the Elementary School Improvement Plan focused on the areas of open response questions and math, particularly
geometry and measurement. At this point in the year, the School Council is proud that we have accomplished many of our
goals. On all professional development half days, Ms. Maria Marolda, educational consultant at Children's Hospital, has provided
additional math training to all teachers and support staff in grades K-4.
We are pleased to report that our Reading Assessment practice has also been re-evaluated and revised. Each child in grades 1-
4 will be evaluated twice each year in reading; in October and March. New Individual reading folders have been Implemented to
document student progress as well as strategies for improvement.
The Passios Elementary School Council meets the second Wednesday of every month to discuss school issues and to develop
its annual School Improvement Plan. Parents and community members are welcome to attend. Members of the Council for the
2002- - 2003 school year are:

In conclusion, we would like to recognize the dedication that retiring grade 4 teacher Miss Louise Morin has given to young
children. At the end of this school year, Miss Morin will retire with thirty-six years of service, all of which were devoted to the
children of Lunenburg. Her dedication to young children, the total school, and the staff has earned Miss Morin the respect and
admiration she deserves. We will miss Louise, but wish her many long, healthy, and busy retirement years ahead.

BOARD OF ASSESSORS
The Board of Assessors and its staff are responsible for the valuation of all real estate and personal property subject to taxation.
Assessed valuations are based on "full and fair cash value" as of the January 1st preceding each fiscal year.
Our office requires a substantial amount of information In order to carry out its valuation mandate. Much of the office's effort
centers around maintaining ownership records, tax maps, and property characteristic data. This data is constantly changing.
Fiscal Year 2002 saw a significant increase in new building site approvals, home construction, and improvements to the existing
housing stock. The resulting increase in inspection activity, time spent in setting up new records, maintaining existing data, and
dealing with all of the political and economic implications brought on by growth kept the office unusually busy.
In an effort to enhance the Town's Geographic Information System (GIS), the office went out to bid to have new aerial
photography and digital orthophotography prepared. The contract was awarded to Kucera International, Inc. of Willoughby,
Ohio. Kucera completed the fly-over in February, 2002 and delivered the orthophotography in April. The digital orthography will
serve as the foundation for a complete re-map of Lunenburg.
Our department saw no changes to the composition of Board of Assessors. At their May meeting, the Board voted Chris
Comeau to serve as Chairman. In July, Norma Frost tendered her resignation, desiring to undertake new opportunities. She
agreed to continue on with the office for up to 2 years in order to train a new Administrative Assessor and oversee a smooth
transition. In October, the Board appointed Barbara LeFebvre. Continuing to serve the Board are Dorothy Musgrove as Principal
Clerk, Janet Leavitt as Inspector, and Harald Scheid as Regional Tax Assessor.
We thank the Town for its support of our office and the efforts we have made to insure that tax revenues flow to the Town in a
consistent and timely manner.
Respectfully,
Harald M. Scheld, Regional Tax Assessor
For the members of the Board of Assessors & Staff
Norma Frost and Barbara LeFebvre, Administrative Assessors
Dorothy Musgrove, Principal Clerk
Janet Leavitt, Inspector
Chris Comeau, Chairman
Paul Dickey, Member
Fred Liatsis, Member

FOLLOWING ARE THOSE RECORDS OF BIRTHS, MARRIAGES, AND DEATHS THAT EITHER OCCURRED IN OUR TOWN OR RECORDS
WHICH WERE FORWARDED TO THIS TOWN FROM OTHER MUNICIPALITIES IN THE STATE. LUNENBURG RESIDENTS ARE URGED TO
NOTIFY THE TOWN CLERK IF IN-STATE RECORDS HAVE NOT BEEN RECEIVED AND RECORDED. IF A BIRTH, MARRIAGE, OR DEATH
OCCURRED OUTSIDE THE STATE, THE SAME MAY BE RECORDED IN THE LUNENBURG TOWN CLERK'S OFFICE IF CERTIFIED
RECORDS ARE PRESENTED. IN SOME CASES THE TOWN CLERK'S RECORDS ARE SUPPLEMENTED BY RECORDS OF BURIALS
WHICH APPEAR ELSEWHERE IN THIS REPORT. IN ORDER FOR THESE RECORDS TO ALSO BE INCLUDED IN THE OFFICIAL VITAL
STATISTICS RECORDS, THE FAMILY OF THE DECEASED SHOULD PRESENT A CERTIFIED COPY OF THE DEATH CERTIFICATE TO THE
TOWN CLERK. IN ACCORDANCE WITH THE GENERAL LAWS, MARRIAGES WHICH OCCUR HERE BUT RELATE TO LICENSES WHICH
WERE OBTAINED ELSEWHERE IN THE STATE ARE NO LONGER INCLUDED IN OUR OFFICIAL TOWN RECORDS.

EXCERPTS:
ANNUAL TOWN MEETING WARRANT 2002
Meeting called to order at 9:00 AM in the Lunenburg High School Auditorium by Moderator, Richard J Bishop, a quorum
was declared present. The Pledge of Allegiance was recited and a moment of silence held for those who died in the past
year. Mrs. Hobbs, 1417 Massachusetts Avenue was recognized and spoke of the 275th anniversary celebration. Mr
David Rodgers, 82 Highland Street was recognized and thanked the following retirees who have collectively given 191
years of service to the Town of Lunenburg; Anthony Falcone, Robert Saudelli, Richard Kullman, Suzanne Cioffi and
Matthew Ryan. Mrs. Jeanne DeBruin, 8 The Clearing was recognized and gave a brief history of the Council on Aging
since its' establishment in 1967. Other retirements mentioned were Fire Chief, Dennis Carrier, Police Officer, Alphonse
Baron, and Building Commissioner, Roger L. Williams. The Moderator led a special thanks to Mr. & Mrs. Bigelow
Crocker for the land donation for the Library. The Annual Town Meeting was adjourned to take action on the Special
Town Meeting warrant.

ARTICLE 10. To see if the Town will vote to re-authorize the establishment of an ambulance billing revolving fund
in accordance with MGL Chapter 44, $53E1/2. The purpose of this fund will be to cover the cost associated with

77
===PAGE 78===
the revenue generated by Advanced Life Support services rendered shall be deposited into said revolving fund. The
proceeds of said Revolving Fund shall be expended by the Fire Department without further appropriation for the sole

revolving fund may not exceed $50,000 during the fiscal year without the approval of the Board of Selectmen and

the following question: "Shall Lunenburg accept Sections 3 to 7 inclusive, of Chapter 44B of the General Laws, as
approved by its legislative body, a summary of which appears below?"
79
===PAGE 80===
Section 3 to 7 of Chapter 44B of the General Laws of Massachusetts, also known as the Community Preservation Act,
establish a dedicated funding source to acquire and preserve open space, parks and conservation land, protect public
drinking water supplies, and scenic areas, protect farm land and forests from future development, restore and preserve
historic properties, and help meet local families housing needs. In Lunenburg, the Community Preservation Act will be
funded by an additional exclse of 3% on the annual tax levy on real property and by matching funds provided by the
state. Property owned and occupied as a domicile by any person who qualifies for low income housing or low or
moderate Income senior housing the Town, as defined in Section 2 of said Act and any other taxpayer receiving an
exemption of real property authorized by Chapter 59 of the General Laws shall be exempt from this act. A Community
Preservation Committee composed of local citizens will make recommendation on the use of the funds and all
expenditures must be voted on by the Town at Town Meeting and will be subject to an annual audit; or take any other
action relative thereto. Submitted by Petition. Board of Selectmen recommendation @ Town Meeting, Finance
Committee recommends disapproval. ARTICLE 25 was PASSED OVER

ARTICLE 29. To see if the Town will amend the Zoning Bylaw by adding to Section 6.0, General Provisions
Affecting All Districts, a new Section 6.7., Demolition Delay To read as follows:
6.7
Demolition Delay
6.7.1
The intent and purpose of this Section is to protect the historic and aesthetic qualities of the Town of
Lunenburg by preserving, rehabilitating or restoring whenever possible, significant buildings or
structures which constitute or reflect distinct features of architectural or historical resources of the Town,
by promoting continued private ownership and utilization of such buildings and by providing owners of
significant structures with time to consider alternatives to demolition, thereby promoting the public
welfare and preserving the cultural heritage of the Community.
6.7.2
Definitions: In addition to the Definitions in Section 2.0. of this Bylaw the following definitions apply to
Section 6.7.
6.7.2.1.
Application: an application for a permit for the demolition of a building, which shall include a photograph
of the building taken within the past year.
6.7.2.2.
Permit: a permit issued by the Building Official for demolition of a building pursuant to an application.
6.7.2.3.
Demolition: the act of pulling down, destroying, removing or razing a building or structure.
6.7.2.4.
Significant Building: any building or structure that the Planning Board determines, in conjunction with
the Historical Commission, Building Official and Fire Chief meets one of the following conditions:
a) Listed on the National or State Registries of Historic Places or is the subject of a pending
application for listing of historic places said registries or,
b) Included in a local registry of Historic Places or is on a current or pending inventory prepared by
the local Historic Commission or,
c) Importantly associated with one or more historic persons or events, or with the broad
architectural, cultural, political, economic or social history of the Town of Lunenburg or the
Commonwealth of Massachusetts or,
d) Historically or architecturally important in terms to period, style, or method of building
construction.
e) And seventy-five (75) years old or more.
6.7.3
Procedure:
6.7.3.1.
An application for a Demolition Permit will be made to the Building Official who shall within five (5) days
forward a copy of the Demolition Permit application to the Planning Board. Said application shall Include
a written narrative that Includes the following:
a) Sketch showing the location of the building or structure with reference to neighboring properties.
b) Photographs
80
===PAGE 81===
c) Description of the age, architectural style, historical association and importance of the building to
be demolished.
d) Reasons for demolishing.
6.7.3.2.
Demolition Plan Review by the Planning Board
a) Within thirty (30) days after receipt of said application, the Planning Board shall hold a demolition
plan review meeting to determine the applicability of the delay procedure or the determination of
non-applicability of the delay procedure. Said meeting will be advertised in the local newspaper
and posted in the usual manner one (1) week prior to said meeting.
b) Written reports shall be presented by the Historic Commission, Building Official and Fire Chief
and the applicant pertaining to the applicability or non-applicability of delay procedure of the
building or structure. The Planning Board shall determine the status of the application based on
the reports noted above. The written report of the determination taken shall be given to the
Building Official within seven days (7) of the closing of the review meeting.
c) If the Planning Board determines the building or structure is not a significant building under this
bylaw, the Building Official may issue a demolition permit upon receipt of the notification.
d) If the building or structure is determined to be a significant building under this bylaw, the
Building Official shall Impose a demolition delay up to one hundred eighty (180) days from the
date of notification, in which time the applicant must notify the Planning Board that a bona fide,
reasonable and unsuccessful effort was made to locate a purchaser to acquire the property at fair
market price for the building or structure and who has agreed to preserve, rehabilitate, restore or
relocate same or has agreed to alternatives to demolition, and or accept conditions noted in the
applicability determination.
e) The owner/applicant shall provide written evidence through documents that attempts have been
made to sell, preserve, move, rehabilitate or restore said building. Evidence shall include a
listing of the building with a real estate broker, an estimate of rehabilitation or restoration of the
structure or an estimate for moving the building.
6.7.3.3.
Emergency Demolition
6.7.3.3.1.
Nothing in this bylaw shall restrict the Building Official or the Fire Chief from ordering the demolition of a
significant building determined to present a clear and present danger to the safety of the public, which
only demolition can prevent.
6.7.3.4.
Non-Compliance
6.7.3.4.1.
The Building Official is empowered to institute any proceedings in the law or equity as he deems
necessary to obtain compliance with the requirement of this bylaw to prevent a violation thereof.
6.7.3.4.2.
No building permit shall be issued with respect to any premises upon which a significant building has
been demolished in violation of this bylaw for a period of two (2) years after the date of the violation.
or take any other action relative thereto. Board of Selectmen recommend approval. VOTED (YES 89 NO
32) (81 needed for 2/3rds majority) as GENERAL BYLAW ARTICLE IX SECTION 22.

Car Wash, provided that

By adding to Section 2.0., Definitions, a new Section 2.1.1.24.,
81
===PAGE 82===
Kennel: Facility for keeping dogs on a single premise, whether maintained for breeding, boarding, sale,
training, or hunting or other purposes and including any premise where dogs are for sale. And
renumber accordingly
And by adding to Section 4.2.2.1. Residential and Outlying, a new Section 4.2.2.1.o) and to Section
4.6.3.1. Commercial District, a new Section 4.6.3.1.K) and number appropriately for each district and
Section 4.6.3.1. renumber the following uses now listed as K-M accordingly.
The new sections to read:
1. A Kennel License shall be issued by the Dog/Animal Control Officer subject to fees established by
the Board of Selectmen according to the following schedule:
One to four (1-4) dogs
Five to ten (5-10) dogs
Eleven to fifteen (11-15) dogs
2. The license period shall be from April 1 to March 31 of the following year.
3. The Dog/Animal Control Officer will inspect the premise where the applicant's kennel will be
located and determine if the premise is appropriate for use and size of the proposed kennel.
4. The Dog/Animal Control Officer will determine if the kennel provides adequate shelter, sanitary
and humane conditions.
5. The Dog/Animal Control Officer will keep detailed records of the kennel operation, will inspect the
licensed kennel at least once a year and/or when a complaint is registered.
6. All dogs shall be kept confined to the kennel and fenced outside runs.
7. The kennel will have no significant adverse effect, such as excessive barking, or other conditions
on the peace and quiet of the neighborhood.
8. The kennel, including outside runs, shall comply to the setbacks as provided in Section 5.0 for
side and rear yards.
Enforcement
For the purpose of this section, irrespective of Section 8.0 of this bylaw, the Building Official shall
designate the Dog/Animal Control Officer to enforce conditions of the permit.
Violations
1. A warning will be issued for the first offense. A violation shall be corrected within 14 days or the
administrator of this bylaw shall issue a citation to the owner of the kennel and file the citation
with the Building Official and forward a copy to the Town Clerk. A twenty-five (25) dollar fine will
be levied for a second violation notice. If the violation has not been corrected to the satisfaction
of the Dog/Animal Control Officer, the fine will be levied for each day thereafter.
2. If the violation issue has not been resolved, the administrator of this Bylaw shall request that the
Building Official forward copies of the citations to the District Court under the provision c40s21d
of the MGL and/or request a hearing before the Special Permitting Authority to revoke the Special
Permit.
or take any other action relative thereto. Board of Selectmen recommend approval. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY

apply for a Kennel License.

4.4.3.6. Bed and Breakfast, Bed and Breakfast Establishment, Inn, Function Facility, Catering Service.
1. Bed and Breakfast, or Bed and Breakfast Establishment provided that:
a) The use complies with the definitions of Bed and Breakfast, or Bed and Breakfast Establishment
providing the dwelling is owner occupied.
b) The lot conforms to all dimensional requirement of this Zoning Bylaw.
c) Parking for each room available for rent is provided in the side yard behind the setback line or in the
rear yard but no nearer than ten (10) feet of any property line. One (1) parking space shall be provided for
each bedroom.
d) The outside appearance of the building is not altered except to comply with the Building Code.
2. Inn, including a Function Facility and Dining Facilities open to the public, provided that:
a) The lot conforms to all dimensional requirements of this Zoning Bylaw.
b) The Function Facilities and Dining Facilities open to the public and Catering Services on the
premises meet the requirements of Sections 3 and 4 listed below.
c) The sleeping rooms do not exceed twelve (12) In number.
d) The parking complies with Section 6.1. Off Street Parking and Loading Areas of the Zoning Bylaw
and shall be provided in the side yard behind the setback line or in the rear yard, but no nearer than
ten (10) feet to any property line.
e) Dining Facilities shall be limited within the time and hours approved by the Special Permit.
3. Function Facility, an accessory to uses permitted in Sections 4.4.3.6.1. and 4.4.3.6.2. to serve as a facility for
meetings and other functions to include reception, dinners, weddings and business and civic meetings and similar
social affairs subject to all provisions of Sections 4.4.4. and 8.4.5. and further provided that:
a) The building is located on a lot at least two (2) acres.
b) The original structure plus accessory buildings devoted to the use shall contain at least three
thousand (3000) square feet of floor area.
c) Additions to the floor areas, if any, shall not exceed fifty (50) percent of the existing floor area and
shall conform to the residential architectural style of the existing building.
d) The number of persons attending events shall be determined by the maximum number allowed by
Building Code, the Board of Health Regulations and/or the Sewer Commission Permit.
e) The number of events in any calendar year shall be determined during the Development Plan Review
process.
f) Anticipated parking shall be provided and parking of over fifty (50) vehicles may require a traffic
policeman at the discretion of the Police Department.
g) Outside lighting or music, if any, shall not extend beyond ten (10) PM and shall be limited in so far as
possible to the property on which the use is located. No lighting shall be reflected in adjacent abutter's
property. The level of sound shall be no higher than seventy (70) decibels at the property line or beyond.
h) Tents or suitable enclosures to be used in connection with an event shall be located back of the
setback line and shall be erected and removed within twenty-four (24) hours of the beginning and end of
the event unless specified in the Special Permit.
4. Catering Service
a) Kitchen and cooking facilities, as an accessory to uses permitted in Sections 4.4.3.6.1. and 4.4.3.6.2.
on the premise and for off site catering must meet the Board of Health Regulations and/or any other
pertinent permitting requirements.
b) Any vehicle Identified for business used in connection with making catering deliveries must be
garaged or parked in the rear of the structure.

ARTICLE
33. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Zoning Bylaw Section 4.0., Use Regulations, by adding a
new Section 4.12., as follows:
4.12.
Lake Whalom Overlay District
4.12.1.
Purpose
4.12.1.1.1 The purpose of the Lake Whalom Overlay District is to provide for the coordinated development
of the former amusement park area and its adjacent area in a manner which will protect the historic
features of the area, provide housing, encourage sound economic development, protect the adjacent
natural features and Town properties, all with minimum environmental and traffic impacts.
4.12.2. Location
83
===PAGE 84===
4.12.2.1 The Lake Whalom Overlay District includes the area bounded as follows: by the present
Commercial District boundary line on the west from the Leominster City line to the center line of
Whalom Road, easterly along the center line of Whalom Road, continuing along the center line of
Pond Street to Kimball Street, southeasterly along the center line of Kimball Street to the center line
of Prospect Street, northwesterly along the center line of Prospect Street to the shore line of Whalom
Lake, southwesterly along the shore line of Whalom Lake to the Commercial District boundary line,
southerly along the Commercial District boundary to the Leominster City line to the point of
beginning.
4.12.3
Permitted uses
4.12.3.1.All uses of the underlying districts, subject to the restrictions and provisions of the under lying
district except for those uses permitted by Section 4.2.3.1.a) 4.6.3.1.j.),I) and m).
4.12.4. Uses Permissible by Special Permit granted by the Planning Board.
4.12.4.1. Town houses, and garden apartment limited to four (4) units per structure.
4.12.4.2Recreation facilities limited to lake front facilities, including a club house, beach, tennis,
badminton, bocce, shuffleboard, gardens, outdoor seating and similar uses.
4.12.5
Conditions of Use
4.12.5.1.All uses are subject to the provisions of Section 8.4.
4.12.5.2. The dimensional requirements of Section 5.0. can be modified by the Planning Board In
connection with a Special Permit or a Development Plan Review, if
a) the development plan is for more than ten (10) acres, and
b) the use or uses are connected to municipal sewer and
c) at least ten (10) percent of the residential portion, if any, is limited in perpetuity to
occupancy by persons fifty-five (55) years or more of age or qualifying for affordable units as
defined by the Commonwealth.
And provided that
a) the density shall not be more than eight (8) units per acre, and
b) there shall not be more than two (2) bedrooms per unit.
4.12.5.3. Consideration shall be given to preserving some of the previous character of the area as an
amusement park, such as signing or markers, maintaining a feature of the park or other
historic structures.
or take any other action relative thereto. Board of Selectmen recommend approval. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY

ARTICLE 9. To see if the Town will vote to raise and appropriate the sum of $75,000 for capital expenditures and to authorize
the Board of Selectmen to petition the General Court for Special Legislation to allow for the establishment of a Capital
Improvement Fund and a Bond and Note Proceeds Investment Fund and; contingent upon passage of such special legislation, to
authorize the Board of Selectmen to deposit said $75,000 appropriation into said Capital Improvement Fund, or take any other
action relative thereto. Board of Selectmen and Finance Committee recommend approval. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY
AN ACT AUTHORIZING THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG TO ESTABLISH A CAPITAL IMPROVEMENT TRUST FUND AND A BOND
AND NOTE PROCEEDS INVESTMENT FUND.
SECTION 1. Notwithstanding any general or special law to the contrary, the Town of Lunenburg may establish and maintain a
special account known as the Capital Improvement Trust Fund, and may raise and appropriate money therefor.
SECTION 2. The Town of Lunenburg may appropriate to the fund by majority vote at an annual or special town meeting in any
year any amount not to exceed 10 per cent of the amount raised in the preceding year by taxation of real estate and tangible
personal property. The aggregate amount of the fund at any time shall not exceed 10 per cent of the equalized valuation of said
town as defined in section 1 of chapter 44 of the General Laws.
SECTION 3. The Town of Lunenburg may, by a By-Law enacted by a 2/3 majority vote at an annual or special town meeting,
direct that a fixed percentage of the Town's "Free Cash" as certified by the Department of Revenue in any given fiscal year and a
fixed percentage, not to exceed 10%, of any new growth in tax revenue as certified by the Bureau of Local Assessment in any
given year, be deposited in said Capital Improvement Fund.
SECTION 4. The fund shall be maintained by the Town of Lunenburg as a separate account. The treasurer of the town shall be
the custodian of the fund and may Invest the funds in such separate account in the manner authorized by sections 55 and 55A of
chapter 44 of the General Laws. Any interest earned on the fund shall be credited to and become part of such separate account.
SECTION 5. The Town of Lunenburg may appropriate by majority vote at any annual or special town meeting any principal and
Interest within the fund for any capital purchase or principal debt payment for any capital purchase. Capital purchase is defined
as any item with a life expectancy of more than 5 years.
SECTION 6. Notwithstanding section 53 of chapter 44 of the General Laws or any other general or special law to the contrary, the
Board of Selectmen of the Town of Lunenburg may establish a special fund or funds in the town treasury into which shall be
deposited income derived from the investment of the proceeds of all notes and bonds issued for any capital building project and
funded through a Proposition 21/2 debt exclusion for the renovation, reconstruction, construction and original equipping of said
town's buildings. The town treasurer shall be the custodian of these monies and shall make an accounting of the funds to the
Board of Selectmen. Except as provided for In Section 7, any amount in such special fund shall be applied solely to the payment
of capital costs or debt service for the building project that the money was borrowed for and no amount shall be spent on such
projects in excess of the amount appropriated therefore without an additional appropriation by town meeting and upon
authorization by the town treasurer.
SECTION 7. If, upon declaration of the town department or committee responsible for oversight of a project referenced herein
that the project is completed, there remains a balance in the special fund attributable to that project, that balance shall be
transferred to the General Fund of the town.
SECTION 8. This act shall take effect upon its passage.

ARTICLE 13. To see if the Town will vote to authorize the Board of Selectmen to petition the General Court for special
legislation to allow for the conveyance of a parcel of land owned by the Town of Lunenburg consisting of 1.7± acres
and identified on the Assessor's Map 64, Lot 404, and recorded in the Worcester North Registry of Deeds, Book 1264
Page 420 to Erling Hanson and Lucy H. Hanson and vote to accept in exchange for this conveyance a deed to a parcel
of land owned by Erling Hanson and Lucy H. Hanson and identified on the Assessor's Map 22 as Lot 1 and consisting of
38± acres and recorded in the Worcester North Registry of Deed Book 706 Page 79.
The exact metes and bounds of both parcels shall be described in deeds to be approved by Town Counsel prior to
recording and the conveyances are subject to enabling legislation from the General Court, or take any action relative
thereto. Board of Selectmen and Finance Committee recommend approval. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY

QUESTION 1: Law Proposed by Initiative Petition
Eliminating State
Personal Income Tax
WHAT YOUR VOTE WILL DO
A YES VOTE would eliminate any state personal income tax for income or other gain realized on or after July 1, 2003.
A NO VOTE would make no change in state tax laws.

STATE ELECTION (continued)
QUESTION 2: Law Proposed by Initiative Petition
English Language
Education in Public Schools
WHAT YOUR VOTE WILL DO
A YES VOTE would require that, with limited exceptions, all public school children must be taught English by being taught all
subjects in English and being placed in
English language classrooms.
A NO VOTE would make no changes in English language education in public schools.

QUESTION 3: Non-binding Advisory Question
Taxpayer Funding
for Political Campaigns
WHAT YOUR VOTE WILL DO
A YES VOTE would advise that the voters favor taxpayer money being used to fund political campaigns for public office in the
Commonwealth of Massachusetts.
A NO VOTE would advise that the voters do not favor taxpayer money being used to fund political campaigns for public office in
the Commonwealth of Massachusetts.
